## God's Timing (Esther)
**Published:** 2026-10-01 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/10/the-timing-of-god-esther.html)**

---

​ Last week, we talked about Nehemiah...

Today, we will be moving a little backwards chronologically, but only by thirty years or so, in order to come across the book of Esther.

Interestingly enough, the book of Esther never names God.

What I like about this book, and something I had never noticed before, is the timelines of God demonstrated throughout its entirety.

The book starts out telling us about King Ahasuerus, who reigned from India unto Ethiopia (Esther 1:1).

We learn that the king is throwing a party with all of his mates, when he calls upon his wife, Queen Vashti, to show herself before him and all of his friends. The king is thrown off guard when the queen responds by saying “No” (Esther 1:12).

I'd imagine, if I were the king and had been embarrassed in a patriarchal society such as this one, I'd be fuming. King Ahaseurus was just that, and Queen Vashti paid the price. Not only did she lose her title as queen, but rabbinic tradition states that Vashti was executed as well (though make note that the text of the Bible does not tell us this).

Without getting graphic, some scholars believe that King Ahasuerus was asking for his wife to appear unto him and his friends wearing only her crown. Given that explanation, her response makes a lot of sense and her situation can be categorized as unjust, for lack of a better word.

However, these circumstances carry us further into the divine Will of God.

After four years of being divorced from Vashti, the king and his servants wanted to find someone to take her place as queen (Esther 2:1-4).

A Jewish man named Mordecai heard this and relayed it to his orphaned cousin, Esther, who was in his custody (Esther 2:5-7).

***

Fun fact: Esther wasn't actually her Hebrew name, but was instead her Persian name. Esther meant "star" but her Hebrew name, Hadassah, meant myrtle, as in a myrtle tree. Taking a look at Isaiah 55:13, it reads,

[Isa 55:13 KJV] – 13 Instead of the thorn shall come up the fir tree, and instead of the brier shall come up the myrtle tree: and it shall be to the LORD for a name, for an everlasting sign [that] shall not be cut off.

This verse will begin to be even more powerful as we explore the life of Esther.

***

Returning back to our account, Moredcai commands Esther to hide her true identity when arriving at the palace (Esther 2:10), likely to aid in the chance of her becoming queen.

We learn that after all these women approach King Ahaseurus, it is Esther who finds favor with him (Esther 2:15). The royal crown is then placed upon her head and she becomes queen (Esther 2:17).

As chapter 2 comes to an end, we read that Mordecai overheard some of the king's chamberlains, or his high ranking officers who likely managed his assets, and they were plotting to kill the king. Mordecai then tells Esther who tells the king, while crediting her cousin as the source of the information.

Chapter 3 opens with the result of what happened next. The king promoted a man to be above all the princes, but that man wasn't Mordecai despite his good character, it was instead Haman (Esther 3:1), a man who hated Mordecai.

The king commanded that all the servants within the king's gates bow and revere Haman, but Mordecai refused to bow down to anyone who wasn't the Almighty. The people, they didn't understand why Mordecai refused to bow. When Haman found out, he grew angry. Learning that Mordecai and his people were Jews, Haman sought to kill all Jews.

Haman appeals to the king and is able to convince the king to let him sign off on a decree that these people who refuse to bow down to the king and follow his commandments be killed (Esther 3:8-11).

***

Another little side note here: the wording of the decree in Esther 3:13 says,

[Est 3:13 KJV] – 13 And the letters were sent by posts into all the king's provinces, to destroy, to kill, and to cause to perish, all Jews, both young and old, little children and women, in one day, [even] upon the thirteenth [day] of the twelfth month, which is the month Adar, and [to take] the spoil of them for a prey.

[Est 3:13 KJV] – 13 And the letters were sent by posts into all the king's provinces, to destroy, to kill, and to cause to perish, all Jews, both young and old, little children and women, in one day, [even] upon the thirteenth [day] of the twelfth month, which is the month Adar, and [to take] the spoil of them for a prey.

These verbs used (destroy, kill, and cause to perish) are interesting, especially when read alongside John 10:10, which reads,

[Jhn 10:10 KJV] – 10 The thief cometh not, but for to steal, and to kill, and to destroy: I am come that they might have life, and that they might have [it] more abundantly.

[Jhn 10:10 KJV] – 10 The thief cometh not, but for to steal, and to kill, and to destroy: I am come that they might have life, and that they might have [it] more abundantly.

The Enemy wants nothing more than to destroy and annilate everything Godly from the face of the earth. Which is powerful in contrast to God the Creator and His Word which is the Seed planted in our lives and the Bread that sustains our every need.

***

Continuing with our story of Esther, we find that Mordecai realizes what has been done and he began to mourn (Esther 4:1). He then meets up with Esther and tells her what has transpired. Mordecai then convinces Esther to go before the king and ask him to undo the decree that Haman had made against her and her people (Esther 4:13-14).

Esther is afraid because she knows that going before the king without being summoned could lead to her execution. It wasn't unless the king stretched out his golden sceptre that a person could be spared in such an instance. But she remains obedient to her guardian nonetheless and requested that all the Jews fast and pray for her situation (Esther 4:16).

After three days of fasting and prayer, Esther puts on her royal apparel and goes before King Ahaseurus. She finds favor once again with the king and he holds out his sceptre toward her (Esther 5:1-2). She asks that a banquet be held and that the king and Haman would attend so that she can make her request before them all there (Esther 5:4).

If you're Haman in this situation, you would probably feel great about the situation. You've just been promoted and now the queen is hosting a banquet for you and the king. The Bible says that Haman is walking around joyful and with a glad heart, but when he spots Mordecai, he is filled with indignation (Esther 5:9), or anger. So Haman, his wife, and all his friends decided that gallows should be made and that Mordecai be hanged thereon (Esther 5:14).

Before the banquet, the king is having trouble sleeping and desires that the book of records be read to him. It is within those records that the king is reminded of Mordecai being the one who warned Esther of the assainantion plots against the him. The king asks what has been done to honor Mordecai for his loyalty and his servants reply that nothing has been done (Esther 6:1-3).

Allow me to read from Esther 6:4-10,

[Est 6:4-10 KJV] – 4 And the king said, Who [is] in the court? Now Haman was come into the outward court of the king's house, to speak unto the king to hang Mordecai on the gallows that he had prepared for him. 5 And the king's servants said unto him, Behold, Haman standeth in the court. And the king said, Let him come in. 6 So Haman came in. And the king said unto him, What shall be done unto the man whom the king delighteth to honour? Now Haman thought in his heart, To whom would the king delight to do honour more than to myself? 7 And Haman answered the king, For the man whom the king delighteth to honour, 8 Let the royal apparel be brought which the king [useth] to wear, and the horse that the king rideth upon, and the crown royal which is set upon his head: 9 And let this apparel and horse be delivered to the hand of one of the king's most noble princes, that they may array the man [withal] whom the king delighteth to honour, and bring him on horseback through the street of the city, and proclaim before him, Thus shall it be done to the man whom the king delighteth to honour. 10 Then the king said to Haman, Make haste, [and] take the apparel and the horse, as thou hast said, and do even so to Mordecai the Jew, that sitteth at the king's gate: let nothing fail of all that thou hast spoken.

[Est 6:4-10 KJV] – 4 And the king said, Who [is] in the court? Now Haman was come into the outward court of the king's house, to speak unto the king to hang Mordecai on the gallows that he had prepared for him. 5 And the king's servants said unto him, Behold, Haman standeth in the court. And the king said, Let him come in. 6 So Haman came in. And the king said unto him, What shall be done unto the man whom the king delighteth to honour? Now Haman thought in his heart, To whom would the king delight to do honour more than to myself? 7 And Haman answered the king, For the man whom the king delighteth to honour, 8 Let the royal apparel be brought which the king [useth] to wear, and the horse that the king rideth upon, and the crown royal which is set upon his head: 9 And let this apparel and horse be delivered to the hand of one of the king's most noble princes, that they may array the man [withal] whom the king delighteth to honour, and bring him on horseback through the street of the city, and proclaim before him, Thus shall it be done to the man whom the king delighteth to honour. 10 Then the king said to Haman, Make haste, [and] take the apparel and the horse, as thou hast said, and do even so to Mordecai the Jew, that sitteth at the king's gate: let nothing fail of all that thou hast spoken.

So, Haman thinks that the king is about to honor him so he speaks quite highly about the reward. At the peak of Haman's pride, the king knocks him down by saying, "do even so to Mordecai the Jew."

Fast forward to the bankquet in chapter 7, the king asks Queen Esther on the second day what her petition was, to which she says,

[Est 7:3-4 KJV] – 3 Then Esther the queen answered and said, If I have found favour in thy sight, O king, and if it please the king, let my life be given me at my petition, and my people at my request: 4 For we are sold, I and my people, to be destroyed, to be slain, and to perish. But if we had been sold for bondmen and bondwomen, I had held my tongue, although the enemy could not countervail the king's damage.

When the king asks Esther who the enemy she spoke of was, she replies, " **The adversary and enemy [is] this wicked Haman** " (Esther 7:6).

The king, now full of anger, the same emotion that Haman held against Mordecai, is told that Haman had been constructing gallows to hang Mordecai on. The king then sentences Haman to the punishment that he had planned to use on Mordecai.

This book is filled with so much irony. Queen Vashti was killed for not appearing before the king when he summoned her. Meanwhile, Esther and her people were spared for her appearance without being summoned by the king.

Additionally, Haman was killed in the gallows he planned to use on Mordecai after being humiliated when he accidentally blessed Mordecai with honor and riches he assumed were for himself.

Scholars believe that if Haman’s plan of genocide had come to pass, the lineage through which Jesus was born would have been wiped out.

Because of the unique timing of Esther taking the throne, her people were spared. The good were blessed, and the evil were punished.

I want to discuss the timliness of God a little more as we transition into small groups.

Questions

Is there an instance you can think of where you discovered the timeliness of God?

Just before coming to CR this evening...

Yesterday's lesson plan...

Why do you think Haman was killed using the gallows he intended to kill Mordecai with?

The golden rule, treat others as you want to be treated (Matthew 7:12).

References

References

[Mat 7:12 KJV] – 12 Therefore all things whatsoever ye would that men should do to you, do ye even so to them: for this is the law and the prophets.

[Mat 7:12 KJV] – 12 Therefore all things whatsoever ye would that men should do to you, do ye even so to them: for this is the law and the prophets.

[Mat 7:12 KJV] – 12 Therefore all things whatsoever ye would that men should do to you, do ye even so to them: for this is the law and the prophets.

## Don't Come Down (Nehemiah)
**Published:** 2026-09-24 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/08/dont-come-down.html)**

---

Last class we talked about Elijah and Elisha. I made mention of the importance to disciple others and grow together in the lifestyle of Christ.

Tonight, still moving chronologically, I want to talk about God's people post-Babylonian exile. There's a lot to say about the exile, but I'll save that for a later message. We will specifically be referencing a man by the name of Nehemiah.

At the end of the Babylonian Captivity, the Persian Empire had overthrown the Babylonians. Instead of being captives, the Israelites were given the choice of whether to return home or continue living their lives in a foreign country.

You might think that everyone had decided to return home, but it was a split choice. Many people packed up their things and returned to Jerusalem to start building (these accounts can be read from the Book of Ezra).

Nehemiah's family were among those who decided to stay and be a part of the Persian society. This led to Nehemiah growing into a position within the royal court– he was the cupbearer for King Artaxerxes I of Persia.

One day, Nehemiah is saddened after hearing what has become of Jerusalem. Never before being sad while delivering wine before King Artaxerxes, the king questions Nehemiah's demeanor. The man is scared, but trusts that the LORD's hand is upon him when he says,

[Neh 2:5-6 KJV] 5 And I said unto the king, **If it please the king, and if thy servant have found favour in thy sight, that thou wouldest send me unto Judah, unto the city of my fathers' sepulchres, that I may build it. 6 And the king said unto me, (the queen also sitting by him,) For how long shall thy journey be? and when wilt thou return? So it pleased the king to send me; and I set him a time.**

[Neh 2:5-6 KJV] 5 And I said unto the king, **If it please the king, and if thy servant have found favour in thy sight, that thou wouldest send me unto Judah, unto the city of my fathers' sepulchres, that I may build it. 6 And the king said unto me, (the queen also sitting by him,) For how long shall thy journey be? and when wilt thou return? So it pleased the king to send me; and I set him a time.**

So Nehemiah goes on his way back to the land of his people, and the king had sent letters unto all the governors to aid Nehemiah on his journey. There were a few of these men who did not agree with the work that Nehemiah wanted to perform. Sanballat, Tobiah, and Geshem all had political territories that bordered Jerusalem, so they viewed Nehemiah as a threat to their power in governement.

They go as far to question his integrity by saying, " **What is this thing that ye do? will ye rebel against the king?** " (Nehemiah 2:19). By questioning Nehemiah's motives, they could try and turn the support of King Artaxerxes away from him. Nehemiah had this to say to them,

[Neh 2:20 KJV] 20 Then answered I them, and said unto them, **The God of heaven, he will prosper us; therefore we his servants will arise and build: but ye have no portion, nor right, nor memorial, in Jerusalem.**

[Neh 2:20 KJV] 20 Then answered I them, and said unto them, **The God of heaven, he will prosper us; therefore we his servants will arise and build: but ye have no portion, nor right, nor memorial, in Jerusalem.**

Nehemiah makes sure that these men realize he holds no political agenda but is instead doing what God, the Ruler over heaven and earth– who is above not just these governors but King Artaxerxes himself– has called him to do.

When we read the Book of Nehemiah, we can see that after assessing the damage that has been done both physically and politically to the land of his people, that the initial time he and the king had arranged would not be enough to create the change that Jerusalem needed. Nehemiah was then appointed governor for the next twelve years according to chapter five of that same book.

There are a few things I want to point out here:

Nehemiah put his title and position on hold in order to do what God was calling him to do. God used the influence Nehemiah had earned to carry out the Will of God. Nehemiah found himself in an elevated position compared to where he was before, doing work that I would argue was more meaningful.

When we follow God and walk by faith, we will found ourselves in places we could never reach on our own. We also can never fully comprehend what God has in store for us, which is why at the end of the day we must not rely so much on five-year plans but rather trust that God has ordered our steps.

Don't misunderstand me and think we don't have to plan because planning and preparation should be used throughout our trusting of God. The LORD will direct us to do wonderful things, to teach, preach, and minister– and no planning alone can get you there, but planning must still take place to be ready for when you arrive.

Transitioning back to our accounts from the Book of Nehemiah, the most famous piece of Scripture, and my favorite verse of all time is this:

[Neh 6:3 KJV] 3 And I sent messengers unto them, saying, I [am] doing a great work, so that I cannot come down: why should the work cease, whilst I leave it, and come down to you?

[Neh 6:3 KJV] 3 And I sent messengers unto them, saying, I [am] doing a great work, so that I cannot come down: why should the work cease, whilst I leave it, and come down to you?

You see, those governors we talked about earlier were still out to prevent Nehemiah's work from coming to pass (which was the LORD's Work). The walls had already been built but that didn't stop them from meddling. Sanballat, Tobiah, and Geshem were prepared to assassinate Nehemiah. They would have been in the wrong to openly kill him and foolish to do try and plan any sort of siege on another governor. These men would have to kill Nehemiah in secret. But with the wisdom and hand of God on his life, Nehemiah says, " **I [am] doing a great work, so that I cannot come down: why should the work cease, whilst I leave it, and come down to you?** "

The enemy is going to try and throw everything he has to at every one of your ministries. It doesn't matter what you aspire to do for the Kingdom– the enemy would rather you spend your time and talent elsewhere. The important thing to remember is this: **Don't Come Down** . Continue to read the Bible, start setting aside time for prayer and fasting– follow those spiritual disciplines that Brother Brody has been teaching on.

No matter what happens, no matter who lets you down or what hurt you may experience in this life, follow the Will of God and trust that He is in control.

Let us take this time and transition into our small groups.

Questions

What works, or ministries, do you feel like God has called you to do?

This doesn't necessarily have to be a calling like those of preachers, teachers, worship leaders, etc., it could simply be a calling of growing closer to God. In this case, what ways do you feel called to grow closer to God and be more spiritually disciplined?

How can we become persistent in the things which we are doing for the Kingdom of God? How can we avoid "coming down" from our great work?

What are some ways we can encourage those around us to continue in the work that God has called them to?

References

References

Nehemiah 2:1-7 Nehemiah 2:19 Nehemiah 6:3

Nehemiah 2:1-7

Nehemiah 2:19

Nehemiah 6:3

## Elijah & Elisha
**Published:** 2026-09-17 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/08/elijah-elisha.html)**

---

Last discussion, we talked about seeking wise counsel. We had discussed Solomon's son, Rehoboam, and the choice he made to listen to the young men he grew up with instead of the elders; his choice led to the seperating of the Southern Kingdom of Judah and the Northern Kingdom of Israel.

Tonight I want to discuss two fellow men: Elijah and Elisha.

Elijah is a prophet famous for confronting King Ahab and Queen Jezebel over their idol worship of Baal. During his time, God held back the rain from the land of Israel, to prove that Baal is not the god of storms that the people think he is.

Later on, Elijah goes up against 450 prophets of Baal to prove that his God, Jehovah, is superior to everyone else's, which he does when God sends fire down from heaven in 1 Kings 18.

Within that same chapter (v. 46), Elijah outruns King Ahab's chariot– this man of God outran a chariot of royal horses, and maintained that speed upwards of 30 miles.

On another occasion, in 2 Kings 1, Elijah calls fire down from heaven and destroys 200 men seeking to do him harm.

Needless to say, Elijah is the man! He is doing Kingdom work, following the obedience of God, confronting false ideologies– we love the prophet!

At one point, we meet our second person in tonight's discussion, Elisha.

[1Ki 19:19-21 KJV] 19 So {Elijah} departed thence, and found Elisha the son of Shaphat, who [was] plowing [with] twelve yoke [of oxen] before him, and he with the twelfth: and Elijah passed by him, and cast his mantle upon him. 20 And he left the oxen, and ran after Elijah, and said, Let me, I pray thee, kiss my father and my mother, and [then] I will follow thee. And he said unto him, Go back again: for what have I done to thee? 21 And he returned back from him, and took a yoke of oxen, and slew them, and boiled their flesh with the instruments of the oxen, and gave unto the people, and they did eat. Then he arose, and went after Elijah, and ministered unto him.

[1Ki 19:19-21 KJV] 19 So {Elijah} departed thence, and found Elisha the son of Shaphat, who [was] plowing [with] twelve yoke [of oxen] before him, and he with the twelfth: and Elijah passed by him, and cast his mantle upon him. 20 And he left the oxen, and ran after Elijah, and said, Let me, I pray thee, kiss my father and my mother, and [then] I will follow thee. And he said unto him, Go back again: for what have I done to thee? 21 And he returned back from him, and took a yoke of oxen, and slew them, and boiled their flesh with the instruments of the oxen, and gave unto the people, and they did eat. Then he arose, and went after Elijah, and ministered unto him.

Elijah takes his mantle and casts it onto Elisha. The young man is well aware what he is being asked to do. A mantle, or a cloak, was very symbolic in that it represented divine calling.

Elijah was famous in the land for being the LORD's prophet. Elisha was a farmer. Neither likely had any encounter prior to this one, Scripture doesn't reference one at least. All we see is that, earlier in this chapter, God sends Elijah to find his replacement.

I want to stop and take a look at Elisha's obedience to the LORD's prophet. History indicates that Elisha was rich, like really rich. Most farmers during his time had maybe one pair of oxen– and that's a maybe– but Elisha had 24 oxen to plow the fields with. Additionally, he had hired servants who were helping him on the farm. But despite all that, to show his commitment unto God, he takes a yoke of oxen, kills them, and boils them for the people to eat. Elisha didn't just kill a pair of ox, he literally burned everything: the wooden yokes, plows, and harnesses.

Elijah and Elisha's relationship is a prime example of the relationships that we should be forming as Christians. Elijah was a mentor for Elisha, the prophet discipled him in order for the LORD's Will to be fulfilled.

[Mat 28:19 KJV] 19 Go ye therefore, and teach all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost:

[Mat 28:19 KJV] 19 Go ye therefore, and teach all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost:

And of course, we know the only name to fulfill those three titles is the name of Christ Jesus.

I want to make note of what the NKJV says, it translates *teach* as *make disciples* .

That's what it takes to make disciples– teaching them about the Word of God and how we can better live our lives by adhering to it.

These two men we're discussing likely spent anywhere from 6 to 8 years together before Elijah was taken up into heaven in 2 Kings 2. What this tells us or should help us understand is that discipling takes time. There isn't necessarily a perfect number that we have for how long discipling should last. I would be hesitant to put a limit on it at all.

There are men and women in my life that I consider myself to be a life-long disciple of– especially the elders of our church, no matter how old and wise I get, they become even older and wiser. Think back on the wise counsel we talked about in the last discussion, it was the elders who made the decision to lessen the burden on the people, which would've prevented a split in the kingdom.

Before Elijah leaves the earth and goes up into heaven, he tells Elisha,

[2Ki 2:9 KJV] 9 ...Ask what I shall do for thee, before I be taken away from thee. And Elisha said, I pray thee, let a double portion of thy spirit be upon me.

[2Ki 2:9 KJV] 9 ...Ask what I shall do for thee, before I be taken away from thee. And Elisha said, I pray thee, let a double portion of thy spirit be upon me.

Scholars say that Elijah performed 8 miracles (or 14 depending on how events are grouped) in his lifetime, while Elisha is recorded to have performed 16 miracles (or 28 under the alternative counting method). Elisha received his double portion.

When Elijah had to leave, Elisha was there to take his place, and the work continued. The same can be said in the New Testament when Paul starts all these churches, he leaves people there to ensure the church continue to grow.

Let's move into our split groups to discuss questions that have been prepared.

Questions

Why do you think Elijah responded so strongly to the calling that had been placed on him?

***This next question is just for self reflection** : Do we feel like we carry out our callings to that same degree?

Why is it important for us to be discipling others? What happens as a result?

How many people should we disciple? Is there a limit to the quantity of people or the quality they may have?

References

References

[Mat 28:19 NKJV] 19 "Go therefore and make disciples of all the nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit,

[Mat 28:19 NKJV] 19 "Go therefore and make disciples of all the nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit,

[Mat 28:19 NKJV] 19 "Go therefore and make disciples of all the nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit,

## Seeking Wise Counsel (Rehoboam)
**Published:** 2026-09-03 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/09/seeking-wise-council.html)**

---

Ice Breaker

I don't have a song to go along with tonight's lesson, but I have several that we can listen to. These songs are similar to the ones we heard with Josiah Queen's "demons," with each artist having their own snippet of vocals. The original song in this case is Forrest Frank's "Jesus is Alive." Let’s start with that one…

Features start @ 1:35

Seeking Wise Counsel

Last class we talked about King Saul and King David. This week, we are going to talk about King Rehoboam, the grandson of David. King Solomon, the son of David and father of Rehoboam, was the wisest of the wise, given the gift of wisdom by God. There were none as wise before him, throughout his time, or even after him have there ever been or will there ever be anyone with such wisdom (1 Kings 3:12).

However, we see that near the end of King Solomon's life, his heart had turned away from God. Solomon's life shows us that no matter how much wisdom we might have, it takes desire and dedication to do what is right before God. I’m sure King Solomon knew the issues that would come out of serving false gods— he was the wisest of the wise after all— but he lacked the conviction necessary to bring change. That’s the thing about sin, the more that we harbor it, the farther we become from God and the less conviction we feel.

But enough about Solomon’s wisdom, let’s talk about his son, Rehoboam.

In 1 Kings 12, we see Rehoboam has just become king. A man by the name of Jeroboam, who had fled from King Solomon, confronts Rehoboam. Jeroboam says,

[1Ki 12:4 KJV] 4 Thy father made our yoke grievous: now therefore make thou the grievous service of thy father, and his heavy yoke which he put upon us, lighter, and we will serve thee.

[1Ki 12:4 KJV] 4 Thy father made our yoke grievous: now therefore make thou the grievous service of thy father, and his heavy yoke which he put upon us, lighter, and we will serve thee.

You see, King Solomon had forced the Israelites into heavy public labor and taxation to build a number of projects over his reign. Jeroboam had wanted the degree of labor and cost from the people to lessen.

So Rehoboam sends Jeroboam away, and begins to seek council. Rehoboam talks to the old men who knew his father. He asked them, "What do you advise me to do?" And they said,

[1Ki 12:7 KJV] 7 And they spake unto him, saying, If thou wilt be a servant unto this people this day, and wilt serve them, and answer them, and speak good words to them, then they will be thy servants for ever.

[1Ki 12:7 KJV] 7 And they spake unto him, saying, If thou wilt be a servant unto this people this day, and wilt serve them, and answer them, and speak good words to them, then they will be thy servants for ever.

Well that's great! Loyalty is something to cherish, especially as a king. This is what I would call a good report, but Rehoboam wasn't satisfied yet. Instead, he was curious what the young men, the people *he* grew up with, had to say. He asked them the same thing, "What would you advise me to do to these people?" To which they replied,

[1Ki 12:10-11 KJV] 10 And the young men that were grown up with him spake unto him, saying, Thus shalt thou speak unto this people that spake unto thee, saying, Thy father made our yoke heavy, but make thou [it] lighter unto us; thus shalt thou say unto them, My little [finger] shall be thicker than my father's loins. 11 And now whereas my father did lade you with a heavy yoke, I will add to your yoke: my father hath chastised you with whips, but I will chastise you with scorpions.

[1Ki 12:10-11 KJV] 10 And the young men that were grown up with him spake unto him, saying, Thus shalt thou speak unto this people that spake unto thee, saying, Thy father made our yoke heavy, but make thou [it] lighter unto us; thus shalt thou say unto them, My little [finger] shall be thicker than my father's loins. 11 And now whereas my father did lade you with a heavy yoke, I will add to your yoke: my father hath chastised you with whips, but I will chastise you with scorpions.

Wow. Those are some heavy words. That's okay, at least now we have both opinions. Seeking out multiple viewpoints isn't a problem, it was actually quite respectable in my opinion for Rehoboam to do. But now the king had a choice to make, and this is what he decided,

[1Ki 12:13-14 KJV] 13 And the king answered the people roughly, and forsook the old men's counsel that they gave him; 14 And spake to them after the counsel of the young men, saying, My father made your yoke heavy, and I will add to your yoke: my father [also] chastised you with whips, but I will chastise you with scorpions.

[1Ki 12:13-14 KJV] 13 And the king answered the people roughly, and forsook the old men's counsel that they gave him; 14 And spake to them after the counsel of the young men, saying, My father made your yoke heavy, and I will add to your yoke: my father [also] chastised you with whips, but I will chastise you with scorpions.

I'm just left here with one thought, *Why Rehoboam?*

It seems a need for power clouded the king's thinking and, as a result, the kingdom was to be divided. David and his family were always to rule over the kingdom, but then it become split into a northern and a southern kingdom. The Northern Kingdom, Israel, would be ruled by Jeroboam while the Southern Kingdom, Judah, would continue to be ruled by David's heirs.

It is interesting to note that throughout this divided kingdom, only 8/20 kings of Judah did right before God. While that statistic may shock you, none of the 19 kings of Israel wholeheartedly followed God. The Kingdom of Israel was dismantled around 700 BC and the Kingdom of Judah ended during the Babylonian exile 100 years after that.

We learn later in 1 Kings 12, when God tells Rehoboam not to fight this split that was beginning to take place, that what was happening was "of the LORD." This meant that God's Will was taking place despite the tragedy that it was– very similar to what we discussed last night in class.

The point I want to make is that while this was God's Will, it doesn't mean we can't take away what is being taught in this passage: wise council is important.

This isn't the only time that we see poor council being sought after in the Bible, just look at the tragedy between Amnon and Tamar. Scripture starts by saying, "But Amnon had a friend..." The story goes that because Amnon loved Tamar, his friend Jonadab convinces him to assault her. Had Amnon went to his father David, he could have married Tamar, but instead he goes down this path that he was never meant to go down– he becomes hateful towards Tamar– all because he put his trust in the wrong people.

It matters who and what we trust.

It matters how we respond when people trust us.

I want to take this time and break into small groups so we can discuss some more of this topic.

Questions

Who or what do you put your trust in most?

Should we put our trust in these people or things?

How are we progressing towards being the type of people who give out wise council? How can we avoid giving unwise council when prompted?

References

References

[1Ki 3:12 KJV] 12 Behold, I have done according to thy words: lo, I have given thee a wise and an understanding heart; so that there was none like thee before thee, neither after thee shall any arise like unto thee.

[1Ki 3:12 KJV] 12 Behold, I have done according to thy words: lo, I have given thee a wise and an understanding heart; so that there was none like thee before thee, neither after thee shall any arise like unto thee.

[1Ki 3:12 KJV] 12 Behold, I have done according to thy words: lo, I have given thee a wise and an understanding heart; so that there was none like thee before thee, neither after thee shall any arise like unto thee.

## The Obedience of a King (Saul & David)
**Published:** 2026-08-06 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/08/obedience.html)**

---

In previous classes, we have talked about both Saul and David, both of whom will be a major focus for tonight's discussion. I want to get started by first discussing Saul.

Saul

Saul was a humble man, at least initially, but his character changed drastically after he received power and authority in his new role as the king of Israel.

We must understand that before Saul, Israel had no king. Before Saul, God led Israel through His prophets. After rejecting God and desiring a physical king to lead them, the Israelites were given Saul to be their first ever king.

Just imagine, the amount of pride that can come with that if one is not careful. Israel had rejected God, so Saul was to fill in, and with all of that in mind, Saul began thinking very highly of himself.

Saul forgot his place with God and power had corrupted his thinking.

[1Sa 13:9-14 KJV] 9 And Saul said, Bring hither a burnt offering to me, and peace offerings. And he offered the burnt offering. 10 And it came to pass, that as soon as he had made an end of offering the burnt offering, behold, Samuel came; and Saul went out to meet him, that he might salute him. 11 And Samuel said, What hast thou done? And Saul said, Because I saw that the people were scattered from me, and [that] thou camest not within the days appointed, and [that] the Philistines gathered themselves together at Michmash; 12 Therefore said I, The Philistines will come down now upon me to Gilgal, and I have not made supplication unto the LORD: I forced myself therefore, and offered a burnt offering. 13 And Samuel said to Saul, Thou hast done foolishly: thou hast not kept the commandment of the LORD thy God, which he commanded thee: for now would the LORD have established thy kingdom upon Israel for ever. 14 But now thy kingdom shall not continue: the LORD hath sought him a man after his own heart, and the LORD hath commanded him [to be] captain over his people, because thou hast not kept [that] which the LORD commanded thee.

[1Sa 13:9-14 KJV] 9 And Saul said, Bring hither a burnt offering to me, and peace offerings. And he offered the burnt offering. 10 And it came to pass, that as soon as he had made an end of offering the burnt offering, behold, Samuel came; and Saul went out to meet him, that he might salute him. 11 And Samuel said, What hast thou done? And Saul said, Because I saw that the people were scattered from me, and [that] thou camest not within the days appointed, and [that] the Philistines gathered themselves together at Michmash; 12 Therefore said I, The Philistines will come down now upon me to Gilgal, and I have not made supplication unto the LORD: I forced myself therefore, and offered a burnt offering. 13 And Samuel said to Saul, Thou hast done foolishly: thou hast not kept the commandment of the LORD thy God, which he commanded thee: for now would the LORD have established thy kingdom upon Israel for ever. 14 But now thy kingdom shall not continue: the LORD hath sought him a man after his own heart, and the LORD hath commanded him [to be] captain over his people, because thou hast not kept [that] which the LORD commanded thee.

We see here that Saul held what we would consider to be a legalist-mindset. Legalism is something we in the Pentecostal-Apostolic movement often get pinned as being. Legalism says, "I'm only doing this because I have to," while the correct approach of relationship says, "I'm doing this to be who God has designed me to be." So instead of focusing on his relationship with God and trusting God to deliver Israel from their enemies, Saul was so concerned with regulations– the way that he believed things were supposed to be done.

We must never make the same mistake that Saul made: to favor regulations over relationship with God. Don't misunderstand me, we *do* have standards and we *do* have holiness principles, but those will all come out of a relationship with God.

This isn't the only time that Saul disobeyed the LORD's prophet, Samuel.

Saul also disobeyed Samuel when he was told to destroy everything in 1 Samuel 15.

Saul brought back king Agag alive along with sheep and cattle.

Saul reasoning was that he believed they could use these animals in sacrifices unto God.

Samuel rebuked him saying,

[1Sa 15:22 KJV] 22 …Hath the LORD [as great] delight in burnt offerings and sacrifices, as in obeying the voice of the LORD? Behold, to obey [is] better than sacrifice, [and] to hearken than the fat of rams.

[1Sa 15:22 KJV] 22 …Hath the LORD [as great] delight in burnt offerings and sacrifices, as in obeying the voice of the LORD? Behold, to obey [is] better than sacrifice, [and] to hearken than the fat of rams.

Here we see Saul had once again missed the point, he held a higher regard for sacrifice than for obedience.

I want to take this time to point back to the song we listened to earlier. It is in Josiah Queen's *Slow Down, Please* where the chorus says,

All the things You do for me

Are good and sweet, but slow down, please

All those things will always be

But sit right here, come and see

There's not a better place to go

To rest your hard and weary soul than at My feet

All the things You do for me

Are good and sweet, but slow down, please

The song highlights that while work is important– especially when it is for the Kingdom– we must also take time for rest and remember that everything we do is an extension of our relationship with God, and that *without* God, the work we do is futile, it’s pointless.

In Scripture, we see that as a result of Saul's rebellious nature, the spirit of God left him.

[1Sa 16:23 KJV] 23 And it came to pass, when the [evil] spirit from God was upon Saul, that David took an harp, and played with his hand: so Saul was refreshed, and was well, and the evil spirit departed from him.

[1Sa 16:23 KJV] 23 And it came to pass, when the [evil] spirit from God was upon Saul, that David took an harp, and played with his hand: so Saul was refreshed, and was well, and the evil spirit departed from him.

Saul, the man who was supposed to be God's chosen vessel to lead his nation, who was promised the Kingdom of Israel forever before he disobeyed God, was now at the mercy of his successor, King David.

God had chosen David to be the next king for Israel.

Saul became so jealous of David that he tried to kill him twice.

Saul spent much time in pursuit of David.

But Saul's life came to an end when he fell upon his own sword in the midst of a battle, unlike his son Jonathan who died fighting.

David

The second king of the united kingdom was David. He was anointed by king Samuel when he was just a young man.

[1Sa 16:13 KJV] 13 Then Samuel took the horn of oil, and anointed him in the midst of his brethren: and the Spirit of the LORD came upon David from that day forward. So Samuel rose up, and went to Ramah.

[1Sa 16:13 KJV] 13 Then Samuel took the horn of oil, and anointed him in the midst of his brethren: and the Spirit of the LORD came upon David from that day forward. So Samuel rose up, and went to Ramah.

Anointing is being able to do the right thing, at the right place, at the right time. For all of those things to happen simultaneously can only be as a result of God’s Will.

When we hear a sermon or a song that resonates with us during moments of our life where we could really use some direction, it may be easy to chalk it up to a coincidence. However, if you haven’t already, you’ll begin to realize the reality that most coincidences are God’s perfect Plan coming together.

Possibly the highest thing that can be said of King David is that although he sinned, he repented because he had a desire to serve God with all his heart.

During one of the Apostle Paul's sermons, he says,

[Act 13:22 KJV] 22 And when... {God had removed Saul}, he raised up unto them David to be their king; to whom also he gave testimony, and said, I have found David the [son] of Jesse, a man after mine own heart, which shall fulfil all my will.

[Act 13:22 KJV] 22 And when... {God had removed Saul}, he raised up unto them David to be their king; to whom also he gave testimony, and said, I have found David the [son] of Jesse, a man after mine own heart, which shall fulfil all my will.

While David was a shepherd caring for sheep, he fought a bear and a lion that threatened his flock. The spirit of the LORD came upon him and he quickly destroyed each animal. On another account, when David went to see how his brothers were doing in the battle against the Philistines, David was enraged by the boasting of Goliath, and by the cowardice of Israel. In Israel’s defense, the enemy, Goliath, stood at over nine feet tall, and he taunted the Israelites and challenged them to fight him. [1Sa 17:3-11 KJV] 3 And the Philistines stood on a mountain on the one side, and Israel stood on a mountain on the other side: and [there was] a valley between them. 4 And there went out a champion out of the camp of the Philistines, named Goliath, of Gath, whose height [was] six cubits and a span. 5 And [he had] an helmet of brass upon his head, and he [was] armed with a coat of mail; and the weight of the coat [was] five thousand shekels of brass. 6 And [he had] greaves of brass upon his legs, and a target of brass between his shoulders. **7 And the staff of his spear [was] like a weaver's beam; and his spear's head [weighed] six hundred shekels of iron: and one bearing a shield went before him. 8 And he stood and cried unto the armies of Israel, and said unto them, Why are ye come out to set [your] battle in array? [am] not I a Philistine, and ye servants to Saul? choose you a man for you, and let him come down to me. 9 If he be able to fight with me, and to kill me, then will we be your servants: but if I prevail against him, and kill him, then shall ye be our servants, and serve us. 10 And the Philistine said, I defy the armies of Israel this day; give me a man, that we may fight together. 11 When Saul and all Israel heard those words of the Philistine, they were dismayed, and greatly afraid.** Although David was a youth, he had faith in God. He speaks quite boldly in 1 Samuel 17:26, [1Sa 17:26 KJV] 26 …What shall be done to the man that killeth this Philistine, and taketh away the reproach from Israel? for who [is] this uncircumcised Philistine, that he should defy the armies of the living God? David used the name of the LORD for his victory in 1 Samuel 17:45-46, where it says, [1Sa 17:45-46 KJV] 45 Then said David to the Philistine, Thou comest to me with a sword, and with a spear, and with a shield: but I come to thee in the name of the LORD of hosts, the God of the armies of Israel, whom thou hast defied. 46 This day will the LORD deliver thee into mine hand; and I will smite thee, and take thine head from thee; and I will give the carcases of the host of the Philistines this day unto the fowls of the air, and to the wild beasts of the earth; that all the earth may know that there is a God in Israel. David used a single stone, *although he picked up five which to me shows humility* , and in the name of the LORD he killed Goliath. Now, we’ve spoken very highly of David, but he was not short of his problems— that's part of why he is called the man after god's own heart. After David sinned via his adulterous act with Bathsheba and the murdering of her husband, God did not overlook it. If the LORD looked past this, then He would not be the Jehovah-tsidkenu (the LORD our righteousness) that we know Him to be. God sent the prophet Nathan to expose David's sin. [2Sa 12:1-7 KJV] 1 And the LORD sent Nathan unto David. And he came unto him, and said unto him, **There were two men in one city; the one rich, and the other poor. 2 The rich [man] had exceeding many flocks and herds: 3 But the poor [man] had nothing, save one little ewe lamb, which he had bought and nourished up: and it grew up together with him, and with his children; it did eat of his own meat, and drank of his own cup, and lay in his bosom, and was unto him as a daughter. 4 And there came a traveller unto the rich man, and he spared to take of his own flock and of his own herd, to dress for the wayfaring man that was come unto him; but took the poor man's lamb, and dressed it for the man that was come to him. 5 And David's anger was greatly kindled against the man; and he said to Nathan, [As] the LORD liveth, the man that hath done this [thing] shall surely die: 6 And he shall restore the lamb fourfold, because he did this thing, and because he had no pity. 7 And Nathan said to David, Thou [art] the man. Thus saith the LORD God of Israel, I anointed thee king over Israel, and I delivered thee out of the hand of Saul;** Despite his action, what makes David the man after God's own heart is verse 13 of that same chapter which has David admitting his wrongdoing when he says, [2Sa 12:13 KJV] 13 …I have sinned against the LORD. And Nathan said unto David, The LORD also hath put away thy sin; thou shalt not die. While Saul failed to be obedient and be the person that God called him to be, David stepped up and took on that role. In the same manner, while Adam and Eve were disobedient in the Garden of Eden, and brought sin into the world, God had a plan to fix it all; He knew that He would one day take on the form of a man to set things right. Sin had entered the world because of mankind and only through a man could it be rectified. Questions Do you ever find yourself caring more about regulations (the way *you* think things should be done) above relationship (the quantity and quality of time you spend with God, which would then reveal how things *should* be done)? Do you ever favor sacrifice over obedience? Do you ever feel you are giving more, perhaps to the result of burnout, instead of simply listening to God’s instructions. How can we be men (or women) after God's own heart? What are some practical things we can do or attitudes we can carry? What are some things that David did which Saul didn't? What must we do when once we have realized our own sins?

While David was a shepherd caring for sheep, he fought a bear and a lion that threatened his flock.

The spirit of the LORD came upon him and he quickly destroyed each animal.

On another account, when David went to see how his brothers were doing in the battle against the Philistines, David was enraged by the boasting of Goliath, and by the cowardice of Israel.

In Israel’s defense, the enemy, Goliath, stood at over nine feet tall, and he taunted the Israelites and challenged them to fight him.

[1Sa 17:3-11 KJV] 3 And the Philistines stood on a mountain on the one side, and Israel stood on a mountain on the other side: and [there was] a valley between them. 4 And there went out a champion out of the camp of the Philistines, named Goliath, of Gath, whose height [was] six cubits and a span. 5 And [he had] an helmet of brass upon his head, and he [was] armed with a coat of mail; and the weight of the coat [was] five thousand shekels of brass. 6 And [he had] greaves of brass upon his legs, and a target of brass between his shoulders. **7 And the staff of his spear [was] like a weaver's beam; and his spear's head [weighed] six hundred shekels of iron: and one bearing a shield went before him. 8 And he stood and cried unto the armies of Israel, and said unto them, Why are ye come out to set [your] battle in array? And he came unto him, and said unto him, **There were two men in one city; the one rich, and the other poor. 2 The rich [man] had exceeding many flocks and herds: 3 But the poor [man] had nothing, save one little ewe lamb, which he had bought and nourished up: and it grew up together with him, and with his children; it did eat of his own meat, and drank of his own cup, and lay in his bosom, and was unto him as a daughter. 4 And there came a traveller unto the rich man, and he spared to take of his own flock and of his own herd, to dress for the wayfaring man that was come unto him; but took the poor man's lamb, and dressed it for the man that was come to him. 5 And David's anger was greatly kindled against the man; and he said to Nathan, [As] the LORD liveth, the man that hath done this [thing] shall surely die: 6 And he shall restore the lamb fourfold, because he did this thing, and because he had no pity. 7 And Nathan said to David, Thou [art] the man. Thus saith the LORD God of Israel, I anointed thee king over Israel, and I delivered thee out of the hand of Saul;**

[2Sa 12:13 KJV] 13 …I have sinned against the LORD. And Nathan said unto David, The LORD also hath put away thy sin; thou shalt not die.

While Saul failed to be obedient and be the person that God called him to be, David stepped up and took on that role. In the same manner, while Adam and Eve were disobedient in the Garden of Eden, and brought sin into the world, God had a plan to fix it all; He knew that He would one day take on the form of a man to set things right. Sin had entered the world because of mankind and only through a man could it be rectified.

Questions

Do you ever find yourself caring more about regulations (the way *you* think things should be done) above relationship (the quantity and quality of time you spend with God, which would then reveal how things *should* be done)?

Do you ever favor sacrifice over obedience? Do you ever feel you are giving more, perhaps to the result of burnout, instead of simply listening to God’s instructions.

How can we be men (or women) after God's own heart? What are some practical things we can do or attitudes we can carry? What are some things that David did which Saul didn't?

## Exodus
**Published:** 2026-07-30 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/07/exodus.html)**

---

Tonight, I want to discuss the Exodus, when Moses led the Israelites out of Egypt.

The word exodus means "a mass departure or going out;" it's an *exiting* of people. I can name several examples of an exodus. In school, when the bell rings, there is a large departure from the classrooms as students flood into the hallways. At the work place, when it becomes time for lunch, many people hit the road and make their way to the nearby restaurants before then departing from there and heading back to work. Even when church gets out, no matter what day it is, we see everyone get in their cars and begin driving away.

The difference between a modern day exodus and the biblical account is that the Book of Exodus describes how the Israelites fled not only from Egypt, but from the slavery and bondage that held them there.

Reading the account, you come across the Ten Plagues, which showed the power of God to the Egyptian people and to Pharaoh. Then, when Pharaoh finally lets the people go, he quickly changes his mind, and the Egyptians chase after the Israelites.

This is where we will pick up with our opening text, in Exodus 14:5-7,

[Exo 14:5-7 KJV] 5 And it was told the king of Egypt that the people fled: and the heart of Pharaoh and of his servants was turned against the people, and they said, Why have we done this, that we have let Israel go from serving us? 6 And he made ready his chariot, and took his people with him: 7 And he took six hundred chosen chariots, and all the chariots of Egypt, and captains over every one of them.

[Exo 14:5-7 KJV] 5 And it was told the king of Egypt that the people fled: and the heart of Pharaoh and of his servants was turned against the people, and they said, Why have we done this, that we have let Israel go from serving us? 6 And he made ready his chariot, and took his people with him: 7 And he took six hundred chosen chariots, and all the chariots of Egypt, and captains over every one of them.

Picking up with verse 11 through 12, we see Israel's reaction to what is taking place,

[Exo 14:11-12 KJV] 11 And they said unto Moses, Because [there were] no graves in Egypt, hast thou taken us away to die in the wilderness? wherefore hast thou dealt thus with us, to carry us forth out of Egypt? 12 [Is] not this the word that we did tell thee in Egypt, saying, Let us alone, that we may serve the Egyptians? For [it had been] better for us to serve the Egyptians, than that we should die in the wilderness.

With Egyptians on one side, and the Red Sea on the other, Israel's doubts began to grow. The exact geographic location of where the crossing of the red sea took place is unknown, but even if the waters were shallow enough for them to wade through, they had women, children, and the elderly to consider. Additionally, the proposed distance between the two sides of the Red Sea range from a few hundred meters to several miles, making it a death sentence for the people to try to cross on their own.

Thankfully, they didn't have to. In verses 13-14 of the same chapter 14 read,

[Exo 14:13-14, 21-23, 27-28 KJV] 13 And Moses said unto the people, Fear ye not, stand still, and see the salvation of the LORD, which he will shew to you to day: for the Egyptians whom ye have seen to day, ye shall see them again no more for ever. 14 The LORD shall fight for you, and ye shall hold your peace. ... 21 And Moses stretched out his hand over the sea; and the LORD caused the sea to go [back] by a strong east wind all that night, and made the sea dry [land], and the waters were divided. 22 And the children of Israel went into the midst of the sea upon the dry [ground]: and the waters [were] a wall unto them on their right hand, and on their left. 23 And the Egyptians pursued, and went in after them to the midst of the sea, [even] all Pharaoh's horses, his chariots, and his horsemen. ... 27 And Moses stretched forth his hand over the sea, and the sea returned to his strength when the morning appeared; and the Egyptians fled against it; and the LORD overthrew the Egyptians in the midst of the sea. 28 And the waters returned, and covered the chariots, and the horsemen, [and] all the host of Pharaoh that came into the sea after them; there remained not so much as one of them.

[Exo 14:13-14, 21-23, 27-28 KJV] 13 And Moses said unto the people, Fear ye not, stand still, and see the salvation of the LORD, which he will shew to you to day: for the Egyptians whom ye have seen to day, ye shall see them again no more for ever. 14 The LORD shall fight for you, and ye shall hold your peace. ...

21 And Moses stretched out his hand over the sea; and the LORD caused the sea to go [back] by a strong east wind all that night, and made the sea dry [land], and the waters were divided. 22 And the children of Israel went into the midst of the sea upon the dry [ground]: and the waters [were] a wall unto them on their right hand, and on their left. 23 And the Egyptians pursued, and went in after them to the midst of the sea, [even] all Pharaoh's horses, his chariots, and his horsemen. ...

27 And Moses stretched forth his hand over the sea, and the sea returned to his strength when the morning appeared; and the Egyptians fled against it; and the LORD overthrew the Egyptians in the midst of the sea. 28 And the waters returned, and covered the chariots, and the horsemen, [and] all the host of Pharaoh that came into the sea after them; there remained not so much as one of them.

The LORD has just performed the miraculous, Israel has been freed from their enslavement and can now follow in the promises of God.

If you are like me, you might be thinking, the amount of faith that must've been built up in each one of the Israelites' life, surely it was enough to last a lifetime. Sadly, this is not the case. In Exodus 16, verses 2-5 we read,

[Exo 16:2-5, 15 KJV] 2 And the whole congregation of the children of Israel murmured against Moses and Aaron in the wilderness: 3 And the children of Israel said unto them, Would to God we had died by the hand of the LORD in the land of Egypt, when we sat by the flesh pots, [and] when we did eat bread to the full; for ye have brought us forth into this wilderness, to kill this whole assembly with hunger. 4 Then said the LORD unto Moses, Behold, I will rain bread from heaven for you; and the people shall go out and gather a certain rate every day, that I may prove them, whether they will walk in my law, or no. 5 And it shall come to pass, that on the sixth day they shall prepare [that] which they bring in; and it shall be twice as much as they gather daily. ... 15 And when the children of Israel saw [it], they said one to another, It [is] manna: for they wist not what it [was]. And Moses said unto them, This [is] the bread which the LORD hath given you to eat. So the children of Israel began to murmur. If you don't know what that word means, it's exactly like it sounds. Murmur... I can't believe God led us out here to die... murmur... I can't believe he expects us to go so long without food... murmur... Egypt was full of food, all of which we were allowed to eat... We know from reading in the New Testament that the LORD knows what we need, in Matthew 6:31-32 Jesus says, [Mat 6:31-32 KJV] 31 Therefore take no thought, saying, What shall we eat? or, What shall we drink? or, Wherewithal shall we be clothed? 32 (For after all these things do the Gentiles seek:) for your heavenly Father knoweth that ye have need of all these things. God knew the Israelites would be hungry, and He is Jehovah-jireh, the LORD will provide. Because of this, He gave the children of Israel what they called *manna* . The word for manna literally means "what is it?" The people did not know what it was, it was a miraculous substance and could only have come from God. Sadly, like the deliverance out of Egypt, this too lost its flavor, pun intended. In Numbers 11:4-6 we read, [Num 11:4-6 KJV] 4 And the mixt multitude that [was] among them fell a lusting: and the children of Israel also wept again, and said, Who shall give us flesh to eat? 5 We remember the fish, which we did eat in Egypt freely; the cucumbers, and the melons, and the leeks, and the onions, and the garlick: 6 But now our soul [is] dried away: [there is] nothing at all, beside this manna, [before] our eyes. This miraculous substance that the LORD provided for His people had become less and less miraculous to the children of Israel. I have said everything tonight to get to this point, firstly, n **ever begin to take the miraculous for granted** . The fact that each one of us is in this place tonight is a miracle. Whether you are taking a look at your life or history itself, you will understand this. I am the son of a non-denominational pastor and I grew up in a church without the fullness of Truth, yet here I am speaking before you about what the Word of God says. We are in this wonderful building that is only a couple years old, it is an extension of our church across the street which was built 87 years ago, in a continent that during the time of Moses would never have even heard of the God of Israel. The second point I want to make is this: **never look back** . 2 Corinthians %;17 tells us, [2Co 5:17 KJV] 17 Therefore if any man [be] in Christ, [he is] a new creature: old things are passed away; behold, all things are become new. The old things are passed away. No longer can we be concerned with that which seemed so glamorous to us in our life before God. In Luke 9:62, Jesus tells the man who wants to be a disciple, but still wants to do things on his own schedule, [Luk 9:62 KJV] 62 And Jesus said unto him, No man, having put his hand to the plough, and looking back, is fit for the kingdom of God. We have been born again after we are baptized in Jesus name and filled with His Spirit, no longer can we focus on the cucumbers, melons, leeks, onions, or garlic of our past, they may seem great now but we forget that we were in slavery during that time. We weren't in a good headspace, we struggled with some things, and that's why we had to be delivered in the first place. We cannot miss the things from before the Red Sea, the things that happened before we looked upon the salvation of the LORD. I'm not saying that we shouldn't testify of what God has done in our lives; we are called to be living testimonies, the salt and light of the earth, but that doesn't mean we miss those things, it means we acknowledge they weren't what God had in store for us. Take it from me, the life after the Red Sea is far superior than the life before it. Let's take this time and break out into split groups and talk about this more. Questions What did your Red Sea experience look like? What happened that made you * go all in* regarding the things of God rather than returning back to the things of this world? Is there anything from your life before Jesus that you find yourself grieving or longing for despite being a new creature in Christ? How can we begin to understand the life we have now with Christ is superior to the one when we didn't have Him?

[Exo 16:2-5, 15 KJV] 2 And the whole congregation of the children of Israel murmured against Moses and Aaron in the wilderness: 3 And the children of Israel said unto them, Would to God we had died by the hand of the LORD in the land of Egypt, when we sat by the flesh pots, [and] when we did eat bread to the full; for ye have brought us forth into this wilderness, to kill this whole assembly with hunger. 4 Then said the LORD unto Moses, Behold, I will rain bread from heaven for you; and the people shall go out and gather a certain rate every day, that I may prove them, whether they will walk in my law, or no. 5 And it shall come to pass, that on the sixth day they shall prepare [that] which they bring in; and it shall be twice as much as they gather daily. ...

## Creation
**Published:** 2026-07-23 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/07/discussing-creation.html)**

---

Tonight, I want to discuss creation.

The subject of creation is a very broad one, we see it occurring everyday. Whether it's the newest iPhone hitting the market, an influencer releasing their latest YouTube video or Instagram post, or even a cat giving birth to her litter of kittens, creation is simultaneously happening throughout our everyday lives.

This world is full of wonder. Surfing the waves of the internet, you will find an exhausting amount of “seven wonders” such as:

Seven Wonders of the Ancient World

New Seven Wonders of the World

...of the Medieval World

...of the Modern World

...of the Industrial World

...of the Engineering World

...of the Christian World

...of the United States

...of Europe

...of Africa

...of Asia

...of Australia

...of South America

...of North America

...of Archaeology

...of Architecture

...of Nature

...of Technology

...Urban Wonders

...Hidden Wonders

...Lost Wonders

The point is, creation is a wonderful thing (at least depending on how it used). AI is an example of creation, it uses prompts to generate an endless number of things.

I’ve said all this to highlight that despite all these wonders, nothing is more wonderful than the creations in which man had nothing to do with. Don't get me wrong, I like to use AI and play video games that were created to fit my preference, or even look at some of those man-made wonders like the Pyramid of Giza or imagine how the Hanging Garden of Babylon would have looked, but all of that falls short when compared to these wonders I am about to show you...

The Natural Wonders of the World

Amazon Rainforest

Hạ Long Bay

Iguazu Falls

Jeju Island

Komodo National Park

Puerto Princesa Subterranean River National Park

Table Mountain

The Geological Wonders of the World

Grand Canyon National Park

Great Barrier Reef (Duplicate)

Mount Everest

Aurora (Northern & Southern Lights)

Parícutin

Victoria Falls

Harbor of Rio de Janeiro

The Wonders of the Underwater World

Belize Barrier Reef

Deep-Sea Vents

Galápagos Islands

Lake Baikal

Northern Red Sea

Palau

Great Barrier Reef

The Wonders of the Solar System

Surface of the Sun

Asteroid Belt

Enceladus

Great Red Spot

Olympus Mons

Rings of Saturn

Oceans of Earth

From the Amazon Rainforest, to the Grand Canyon, to the Great Barrier Reef, to the Surface of the Sun– oh what wonder it is to live in the same universe as all of these things. More importantly, what a wonder it is to know the God who formed each of these things. Throughout the book of Genesis, we see all that God formed and the order in which he did it.

God divided the light and darkness

God placed the firmament in the sky and divided the waters of earth from the waters of heaven

God created land and yielded grass, herbs, and trees from it

God placed the sun, moon, and stars into the sky, inventing days, seasons, and years

God placed fish into the sea, and birds into the air

God created animals and then created us— mankind— in his image

God rested

Throughout the entirety of His process, God declared each one of His creations as good. Despite knowing that we as mankind would fall into sin and struggle with our fleshly temptations, God still called us good.

God spent what could only be conceived by us as a large amount of time and energy into the creation of the world, only to reveal to us in Matthew 24:35,

[Mat 24:35 KJV] 35 Heaven and earth shall pass away, but my words shall not pass away.

[Mat 24:35 KJV] 35 Heaven and earth shall pass away, but my words shall not pass away.

How many of us are thankful for the Word of God? I am so grateful for the Word and the continuity it carries. Throughout the entirety of Scripture, it affirms itself without contradiction. We see this in John's writing of Revelation when it reada,

[Rev 21:1 KJV] 1 And I saw a new heaven and a new earth: for the first heaven and the first earth were passed away; and there was no more sea.

[Rev 21:1 KJV] 1 And I saw a new heaven and a new earth: for the first heaven and the first earth were passed away; and there was no more sea.

John had a vision of the words of Jesus coming to pass, the world as he knew it (and as we know it) had passed away/will pass away. What’s great about this is that we will have the opportunity to move on into life everlasting. Everything that God created during the seven days of creation do not compare to His most favored creation of all– us.

When Adam and Eve sinned, God took animal coats to clothe them from their guilt and shame. You see, unlike those animals, we have been made in the image of God; mankind is the only thing to be made in God's image. We are His most beloved creation, we are the ones that He went to Calvary for and the ones He desires to have a relationship with.

We will expand upon this during our small group discussions. Let’s take this time and transition into those, all of us will circle up.

Questions

Which of God's creations most fascinates you?

Why do you think God formed all of these things (landscapes, animals, planets, etc.); what purpose do they serve?

Why would God create things that are so far out of our reach, such as other planets and even other galaxies?

How does it make you feel that God chose us, mankind, to be the creation in which He carries a relationship with?


---

## Jehovah
**Published:** 2026-07-16 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/07/jehovah-continued.html)**

---

I know we have went over the names of Jehovah both in CR and in Youth class recently, so I want to quickly go over a few that I had not mention in either of those settings. After that, I want to start a new lesson format. Every time I have taught here on Thursday nights, I have called what I was presenting a discussion. However, there has not been very much discussing, largely because there were not very many questions presented to all of you. So moving forward, I hope to present a lot more opinionated questions (having no right or wrong answers) and encourage a lot more feedback. I'm not asking that anyone speak super deeply about the subjects we cover– I'd love if we could get there one day, but baby steps– all I'm asking is that we could all reflect on what the Word of God says, that we could have the opportunity to answer one another's questions, and that we could all grow to edify one another as a result.

That word edify can sound scary, I'd never heard it until I got into Hyphen Class and Sister Mackenzie had taught on it. All edification means is simply, " *to build up* ." The Bible teaches about building others up in both the Old and New Testaments.

One famous Proverb found in the Bible says,

[Pro 27:17 NKJV] 17 [As] iron sharpens iron, So a man sharpens the countenance {or characteristics} of his friend.

[Pro 27:17 NKJV] 17 [As] iron sharpens iron, So a man sharpens the countenance {or characteristics} of his friend.

Paul must have been a big fan of edification because he included it in all three of the following letters to the churches:

[Rom 14:19 NKJV] 19 Therefore let us pursue the things [which make] for peace and the things by which one may edify another.

[Rom 14:19 NKJV] 19 Therefore let us pursue the things [which make] for peace and the things by which one may edify another.

[1Th 5:11 NKJV] 11 Therefore comfort each other and edify one another, just as you also are doing.

[1Th 5:11 NKJV] 11 Therefore comfort each other and edify one another, just as you also are doing.

In other words, Paul tells the church is Thessalonica to continue edifying one another.

[Eph 4:29 NKJV] 29 Let no corrupt word proceed out of your mouth, but what is good for necessary edification, that it may impart grace to the hearers.

[Eph 4:29 NKJV] 29 Let no corrupt word proceed out of your mouth, but what is good for necessary edification, that it may impart grace to the hearers.

Paul instructs us that our words should not bring others down, but it should build them up. I'll be the first to admit I'm not great at taking constructive criticism, it makes me feel *weird* , but so long as it stays constructive, it is good for us to hear, and allows us to grow.

Recapping the compound-names of Jehovah, in the previous CR and Youth classes, we have talked about,

Jehovah-jireh / The LORD will see (i.e., will provide)

Jehovah-rapha / The LORD that heals

Jehovah-nissi / The LORD our banner (i.e., victory)

I made reference to the pillagers in Minecraft that carry the banners and give the "bad omen" effect. A banner is a symbol of power and victory, and Scripture says that the LORD is our banner, meaning He is our victory.

Jehovah-m’kaddesh / The LORD that sanctifies

*God makes us holy for Him* . Sanctification, or holiness, is separation from sin and the things of this world; a life with God creates separation from the things that turn us away from God.

Jehovah-shalom / The LORD our peace

Peace is a fruit of the Spirit, nothing else but the Spirit of God can offer us a genuine peace that lasts.

Jehovah-tsidkenu / The LORD our righteousness

*God makes us right with Him* and will ultimately make all things right in eternity

[Rev 21:4 NKJV] 4 "And God will wipe away every tear from their eyes; there shall be no more death, nor sorrow, nor crying. There shall be no more pain, for the former things have passed away."

Jehovah-shammah / The LORD is present

God is an omnipresent Spirit, meaning He is everywhere all at once.

[Psa 145:18 NKJV] 18 The LORD [is] near to all who call upon Him, To all who call upon Him in truth.

Jehovah-saboath / The LORD of hosts (i.e., almighty) The LORD of Hosts reminds us that God is the Commander of the armies of heaven. Whether He works through His people, His angels, or even the elements of creation, nothing is beyond His command. We have already seen that He is Jehovah-Nissi, our victory. As Jehovah-Sabaoth, He is also the One who leads the battle and accomplishes that victory. Jehovah-elyon / The LORD most high This name points to God's absolute sovereignty. He is the supreme ruler over heaven and earth, and no king, nation, spiritual power, or force of nature is above His authority. Because He reigns over all creation, every victory ultimately belongs to Him. He is not merely victorious in battle—He is the Most High God, whose will cannot be overthrown. Jehovah-raah / The LORD my shepherd Previously, we have discussed 23rd Psalm, I encourage you to read it in its entirety of six verses, it speaks on how the LORD is our Shepherd and we are the sheep, and how He loves and cares for us. Jehovah-hoseenu / The LORD our maker God created us in His image (Genesis 1:27), which means we quite literally have an image to uphold when interacting with others and going about our everyday lives– not only are we caring a great deal about our own image, how people view us, but as Christians we are also concerned with how people view the Creator we accept as our own. I know there was a lot there, I hope the handouts helped and continue to help as we move into the questions... Questions Which of these names describing the character of God most resonate with you and why? Did one of these names surprise you, did they make you realize a characteristic of God that you had not yet considered? How do you feel after now seeing just a few of the roles that God fulfills in our lives?

Jehovah-saboath / The LORD of hosts (i.e., almighty)

The LORD of Hosts reminds us that God is the Commander of the armies of heaven. Whether He works through His people, His angels, or even the elements of creation, nothing is beyond His command. We have already seen that He is Jehovah-Nissi, our victory. As Jehovah-Sabaoth, He is also the One who leads the battle and accomplishes that victory.

Jehovah-elyon / The LORD most high

This name points to God's absolute sovereignty. He is the supreme ruler over heaven and earth, and no king, nation, spiritual power, or force of nature is above His authority. Because He reigns over all creation, every victory ultimately belongs to Him. He is not merely victorious in battle—He is the Most High God, whose will cannot be overthrown.

Jehovah-raah / The LORD my shepherd

Previously, we have discussed 23rd Psalm, I encourage you to read it in its entirety of six verses, it speaks on how the LORD is our Shepherd and we are the sheep, and how He loves and cares for us.

Jehovah-hoseenu / The LORD our maker

God created us in His image (Genesis 1:27), which means we quite literally have an image to uphold when interacting with others and going about our everyday lives– not only are we caring a great deal about our own image, how people view us, but as Christians we are also concerned with how people view the Creator we accept as our own.

I know there was a lot there, I hope the handouts helped and continue to help as we move into the questions... Questions Which of these names describing the character of God most resonate with you and why? Did one of these names surprise you, did they make you realize a characteristic of God that you had not yet considered? How do you feel after now seeing just a few of the roles that God fulfills in our lives?

I know there was a lot there, I hope the handouts helped and continue to help as we move into the questions...

Questions

Which of these names describing the character of God most resonate with you and why?

Did one of these names surprise you, did they make you realize a characteristic of God that you had not yet considered?

How do you feel after now seeing just a few of the roles that God fulfills in our lives?
