## An Abused Anointing
**Published:** 2026-07-02 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/07/an-abused-anointing.html)**

---

Last week, Chris taught us to be intentional with every step we take– worshipping God, no matter who's around or what they may think of us as a result.

Tonight, we'll be talking about a different subject– my discussion is entitled, "An Abused Anointing."

This thought came to me after Brother Kyle's message on Sunday night about Samson.

​The point I hope to make throughout this discussion is twofold. Firstly, God has a calling for each of us here tonight (which is something I strive to always make apparent). Secondly, something that I have yet to shed much light on, is that we have the freedom to ignore, or even abuse our callings from God.

Let's start by understanding God's calling on each of our lives. The famous Jeremiah 29:11 says,

[Jer 29:11 KJV] 11 For I know the thoughts that I think toward you, saith the LORD, thoughts of peace, and not of evil, to give you an expected end.

[Jer 29:11 KJV] 11 For I know the thoughts that I think toward you, saith the LORD, thoughts of peace, and not of evil, to give you an expected end.

There is a dangerous misunderstanding that can arise if not put in check. It can be easy to read this verse and think to ourselves, *I can live however I want because God has an expected end in mind for me* . What we must understand is that while we do have the liberty to do whatever we want in this life, sin separates us from God and all things that are of God. If we want to live out those thoughts that God has for us, we must walk with Him and talk with Him on a daily basis. These next few verses give us more understanding:

[Psa 37:23 KJV] 23 The steps of a [good] man are ordered by the LORD: and he delighteth in his way.

[Psa 37:23 KJV] 23 The steps of a [good] man are ordered by the LORD: and he delighteth in his way.

[Pro 16:9 KJV] 9 A man's heart deviseth his way: but the LORD directeth his steps.

[Pro 16:9 KJV] 9 A man's heart deviseth his way: but the LORD directeth his steps.

[Pro 20:24 KJV] 24 Man's goings [are] of the LORD; how can a man then understand his own way?

[Pro 20:24 KJV] 24 Man's goings [are] of the LORD; how can a man then understand his own way?

When living for God and walking with Him, we are walking a path that has been laid out for us already. God's Will for our lives had been decided long before we were born and even long before existence came to be. I believe that even before the seven days of creation, God saw each one of us and knew His plans for our life. God is omniscient, or all-knowing. God formed Adam and Eve with the knowingness that they were going to fall into sin, as well as with how He would save us from sin.

Now, some believe that because of this, our actions have been predestined and we have no choice in the matter. This is not a belief that we hold, given that it is not biblical. God has given us liberty to make choices in life; while, yes, He already knows the outcome for our lives, it based on the choices that we make that bring us to said outcome.

What I want to focus on tonight is when we are anointed by God.

The word used for *the anointed* in the Old Testament is māšîaḥ (maw-shee'-akh). It not only was used to refer to kings and high priests, but it was also used in reference to the coming Messiah– in which we have the benefit of knowing would be Jesus Christ.

In the Greek, the word for anointed is Χριστός (khris-tos'), or Christ as it appears in English. There is sometimes a misconception that Christ was Jesus' last name, but Christ was more of a title than a name and meant *the anointed one* . You see, Jesus had a calling, He knew that the law would not be sufficient enough to cover our sins, so He came down as a man and paid the price for our sins.

Similarly, in our lives, we have a calling that has been given to us, we have a burden that has been placed on us, and we have a cross we are called to carry.

Matthew 16:24 says,

[Mat 16:24 KJV] 24 Then said Jesus unto his disciples, If any [man] will come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me.

[Mat 16:24 KJV] 24 Then said Jesus unto his disciples, If any [man] will come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me.

I want to talk about a few people who were called by God– they had a cross to carry, they were given opportunities and positions where God was going to use them for the benefit of His people– and yet, they instead decided to abuse what God had given them.

First off, let us talk about Samson. Kyle did a great job last Sunday night telling us about the pride of Samson, so a lot of this will be review. Samson was a judge over Israel, a type of leader before kings had been established in the land. Samson had been committed to a Nazarite vow since birth: never eating anything containing grapes, going near dead carcases, or cutting his hair. As a result, God granted Samson with an unbelievable amount of strength.

Samson was a one man army. Samson had the ability to defeat all of those who opposed the God of Israel, but instead he chose to do what pleased him. Samson chose to toe the line of his walk with God and the things of the world. Samson, I believe, chose to tempt himself by walking through vineyards, eating honey from a dead lion, and fraternize with those who lived in the world and did not accept the God of Israel as the one, true God. Samson abused the power that had been given to him to do whatever he saw fit– he abused the anointing of God for his own gain rather than his Creator's. We must ask ourselves, are we abusing the gifts that God has blessed us with to benefit ourselves instead of His Kingdom? I'll leave that there.

I also want to talk about King Saul. After the Judges, Israel wanted a king and they got King Saul. Initially, Saul did good by following after God and honoring His Word. On his second year as king, however, he began to favor sacrifice over obedience to God. Saul grew impatient waiting on the LORD's prophet to arrive, so he decides to make the burnt offering himself (1 Samuel 13). In this moment, you can see Saul becoming more legalist than relational towards God. He believed that making the sacrifice was more important than doing what His Maker had instructed Him to do. Later, we see Saul disobey God again by sparing King Agag of the Amalekites, Israel's enemy, and all of the spoil in the land even though God explicitly said to destroy everything and save nothing. Saul's excuse was, "I thought we could make sacrifices unto the LORD with the best sheep and oxen from that land," which again points to Saul's misunderstanding of God. Samuel corrects Saul by telling him the LORD favors obedience over sacrifice.

As I was putting this lesson together, I found myself leaning into sacrifice instead of obedience. This was on Tuesday, my parents asked me to grab the mail before leaving the house, but I was running late to prayer. Initially, I said no, and then the words of Samuel the prophet rang in my mind,

[1Sa 15:22 KJV] 22 And Samuel said, Hath the LORD [as great] delight in burnt offerings and sacrifices, as in obeying the voice of the LORD? Behold, to obey [is] better than sacrifice, [and] to hearken than the fat of rams.

[1Sa 15:22 KJV] 22 And Samuel said, Hath the LORD [as great] delight in burnt offerings and sacrifices, as in obeying the voice of the LORD? Behold, to obey [is] better than sacrifice, [and] to hearken than the fat of rams.

The lives of Samson and Saul are interesting because they had both been chosen by God, and yet did not honor Him. Samson did that which was right in his own eyes, he did what he wanted, while Saul did what he *thought* God would want Him to do rather than what God had actually asked of him.

We must avoid both of these mistakes in our callings and anointings from the LORD. We must honor God by doing what is asked of us, and not try to play God by doing what we *think* He might appreciate instead. He is God, and we are humankind. Isaiah 55:8-9 says,

[Isa 55:8-9 KJV] 8 For my thoughts [are] not your thoughts, neither [are] your ways my ways, saith the LORD. 9 For [as] the heavens are higher than the earth, so are my ways higher than your ways, and my thoughts than your thoughts.

[Isa 55:8-9 KJV] 8 For my thoughts [are] not your thoughts, neither [are] your ways my ways, saith the LORD. 9 For [as] the heavens are higher than the earth, so are my ways higher than your ways, and my thoughts than your thoughts.

Let's reflect on what we have talked about here tonight as we enter into a time of prayer...


---

## The Potter
**Published:** 2026-05-28 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/05/the-potter.html)**

---

Who remembers last week's lesson? We played a few songs before our discussion that reflected the topic of...?

* ... *

Before we get started, let's pray...

I want to read from Jeremiah 18:1-6, which says:

' The word which came to Jeremiah from the Lord , saying: “Arise and go down to the potter’s house, and there I will cause you to hear My words.” Then I went down to the potter’s house, and there he was, making something at the wheel. And the vessel that he made of clay was marred in the hand of the potter; so he made it again into another vessel, as it seemed good to the potter to make. Then the word of the Lord came to me, saying: “O house of Israel, can I not do with you as this potter?” says the Lord . “Look, as the clay is in the potter’s hand, so are you in My hand, O house of Israel! ' Jeremiah 18:1-6

' The word which came to Jeremiah from the Lord , saying: “Arise and go down to the potter’s house, and there I will cause you to hear My words.” Then I went down to the potter’s house, and there he was, making something at the wheel. And the vessel that he made of clay was marred in the hand of the potter; so he made it again into another vessel, as it seemed good to the potter to make. Then the word of the Lord came to me, saying: “O house of Israel, can I not do with you as this potter?” says the Lord . “Look, as the clay is in the potter’s hand, so are you in My hand, O house of Israel! ' Jeremiah 18:1-6

In this passage, God begins to show Israel not only his capabilities but also the relationship He wants with them. We can further investigate this when reading this passage from Isaiah 64:8-9:

' But now, O Lord , You are our Father; We are the clay, and You our potter; And all we are the work of Your hand. Do not be furious, O Lord , Nor remember iniquity forever; Indeed, please look—we all are Your people! ' Isaiah 64:8-9 NKJV

' But now, O Lord , You are our Father; We are the clay, and You our potter; And all we are the work of Your hand. Do not be furious, O Lord , Nor remember iniquity forever; Indeed, please look—we all are Your people! ' Isaiah 64:8-9 NKJV

Is there anyone here who does pottery? Okay, instead of pottery, how many here have played with Play-Doh? Sitting at the table, rolling up Play-Doh, and cutting it into shapes is a very fond memory of mine. Which is why I brought some here tonight.

Now, Play-Doh isn't quite the same as clay, but you'll get the same idea as we explore this topic tonight.

' Then God said, “Let Us make man in Our image, according to Our likeness; let them have dominion over the fish of the sea, over the birds of the air, and over the cattle, over all the earth and over every creeping thing that creeps on the earth.” So God created man in His own image; in the image of God He created him; male and female He created them. ' Genesis 1:26-27 NKJV

' Then God said, “Let Us make man in Our image, according to Our likeness; let them have dominion over the fish of the sea, over the birds of the air, and over the cattle, over all the earth and over every creeping thing that creeps on the earth.” So God created man in His own image; in the image of God He created him; male and female He created them. ' Genesis 1:26-27 NKJV

With some more context, chapter 2, verse 7 of Genesis reads:

' And the Lord God formed man of the dust of the ground, and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life; and man became a living being. ' Genesis 2:7

' And the Lord God formed man of the dust of the ground, and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life; and man became a living being. ' Genesis 2:7

Recently, I have talked a lot about this moment in Genesis. The creation of man from the dust of the earth. It's incredibly powerful, you'll have to admit, that before God, we were nothing more than the Play-Doh sat before us. Arguably, we were even less appealing because, as clay or dirt, we wouldn't have had the variety of colors set before you.

Beyond the miracle of being formed from clay, God formed us *in His image* . So no matter how far off we get from the Strait and Narrow– no matter how far we veer from the path of righteousness, we have a place we can return to; we can return to the image that God first created us to have: His image.

I could stand here and tell you all of the reasons I'm not qualified, but to be more productive, I'll settle for saying that God has been so great to me, forming me and shaping me to be more like Him. Even when I struggle to maintain that image, as the Potter, God says, " *Let me correct that* ." Or He'll say, " *Let's start again, let's begin anew* ." The Bible says in 2 Corinthians 5:17, ' **Therefore, if anyone is in Christ, he is a new creation; old things have passed away; behold, all things have become ** **new** . '

It won't always, if ever, be a comfortable process to be made new. When change has to take place, reshaping must happen at the hands of the Potter. The Bible repeatedly calls us to crucify our flesh. Crucifying is exactly what happened to Jesus when He was nailed and killed on the cross, and that same language is how we are supposed to treat our flesh. That's not a comfortable process, but I can promise you that it's a rewarding one. More than the riches of this world, the fame or glory that it will tempt you with, a relationship with the Potter exceeds it all.

I have heard it said that when Jesus was put up on the cross, He was beaten, battered, and bruised– but His bones were not broken. This is a symbol for us and the world. It is a demonstration that while the world will hurt us and knock us down, it cannot break us. Only God, the true potter, can break us– not to harm us, but to shape us. We might feel broken– we might feel empty, but that's only because the Potter is about to reshape us into something that we couldn't be on our own.

If you believe that, if you're thankful for what the Potter is doing in your life, can you just give God the praise right now!


---

## Psalm 23
**Published:** 2026-05-21 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/05/psalm-23.html)**

---

Who remembers last week's lesson about Moses?​

* ... *

Today, as you already know based on tonight's music, we are taking a look at the 23rd Psalm. This is arguably the most famous of all the Psalms, depicting a beautiful understanding, I hope to present tonight.

I love this passage of Scripture and want to challenge myself to memorize the next six verses. I encourage you all to do the same. One reason this Psalm resonates so much with me is that it was my grandma's favorite. I remember one day when she asked me, "Jonny, can you help me? Do you know where the 23rd Psalm is? I can't seem to find it." You can ask Lily, that pretty much sums up Grandma perfectly. The passage reads,

“1The Lord is my shepherd; I shall not want. 2He makes me to lie down in green pastures; He leads me beside the still waters. 3He restores my soul; He leads me in the paths of righteousness For His name’s sake. 4Yea, though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death, I will fear no evil; For You are with me; Your rod and Your staff, they comfort me. 5You prepare a table before me in the presence of my enemies; You anoint my head with oil; My cup runs over. 6Surely goodness and mercy shall follow me All the days of my life; And I will dwell in the house of the Lord Forever.” Psalms 23:1-6 NKJV

“1The Lord is my shepherd; I shall not want. 2He makes me to lie down in green pastures; He leads me beside the still waters. 3He restores my soul; He leads me in the paths of righteousness For His name’s sake. 4Yea, though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death, I will fear no evil; For You are with me; Your rod and Your staff, they comfort me. 5You prepare a table before me in the presence of my enemies; You anoint my head with oil; My cup runs over. 6Surely goodness and mercy shall follow me All the days of my life; And I will dwell in the house of the Lord Forever.” Psalms 23:1-6 NKJV

First and foremost, I want to highlight that God is our Shepherd, meaning that we are His sheep and He loves and cares for us. God wants to protect us from evil and ensure we aren't veering too far from Him.

When you study sheep, you learn that they can discern their shepherd's voice. They spend so much time together that they are comfortable with his presence. That metaphor speaks volumes about how we should feel in the presence of God. It's easy to withhold worship from God, to withhold our time from God, out of fear of what others may think or of the many things we need to get done. However, if we were to draw close to our Shepherd and listen to His Word through both prayer and Bible reading, we would find ourselves ever so comfortable in His presence.

1. A life with God is a life without wants.

2. God will lead us to our needs. Are the fowls of the air not fed? So how much more will we be fed? Are the lilies of the field not clothed? So how much more will we, the children of God, be clothed?

(Matthew 6)

3. God leads us in the way of righteousness. This is the strait and narrow path, an image I so desperately love to talk about. We are called to be holy and separate from the world. God has ordered those steps and is directing us to take them.

(Matthew 7:14; Psalm 37:23)

4. At times, we are going to be in valleys, low points in our lives, where hope seems so far away. Yet, we serve a Comforter who will always be as close as the mention of His name!

The rod represents protection. It was a sturdy stick used to fight off wild animals, such as wolves or lions, that threatened the flock. It was also used to discipline or correct sheep that wandered too far, acting as a "chastening rod".

How many know that just as we need protection from the evil of this world, such as the world and the devil, we also need protection over our flesh?

The staff represents guidance. It was a long, often hooked stick used to gently lead sheep, pull them back from dangerous edges, or lift them from treacherous areas. It allowed the shepherd to guide individual sheep into the proper path.

The LORD is both our Protector and our Guide.

(John 14:26)

5. God brings us peace, even among those who seek our demise. God anoints us, He chooses and calls us just as He called many of the people we have been talking about in previous classes. Just as God called Noah, Abraham, Moses, and the list goes on, He is calling each person in this room. More than talent and ability, He anoints us, which takes us farther than we will ever get on our own. Our God **who is able to do exceedingly abundantly above all that we ask or think** (Ephesians 3:20), He fills our cup in the same manner. A full cup is an abundant cup, but with the LORD our cups will flow over. We can do all things through Christ who strengthens us (Philippians 4:13).

6. This verse is interesting. More than a declaration of who God is or what He can do, this is a declaration from the Psalmist David, a vow that He will remain in the house of the LORD forever. Allow me to be very honest. Each and every one of you has to make up your mind *now* . If we don't make that same vow like David did while we are young, that we will remain in the house of God all the days of our lives, we will find ourselves swept away, out of the house, by the things of this world. Temptations will come as many of you are getting to the age where you can drive and choose where you want to spend your Sundays, Wednesdays, and even Thursdays. We have to make up our minds now to choose to be the house of God. Beyond age 16, once you get to be 21, you have a lot more freedom then too and have to decide to live righteously. We have to say, "I don't care what my friends are doing, I'm choosing holiness. I'm choosing to live in the house of the LORD all the days long."

I want to transition to a different element of tonight's discussion. Before we wrap up, I want to talk about another shepherd we have, and one that God has given us: our pastor.

According to David K. Bernard in his book, *Spiritual Gifts* , a pastor is defined as "one who leads and takes care of God's people." The word pastor is closely related to pasture, a field where sheep graze. Another word for pastor is shepherd, which aptly describes the role.

I'm thankful that we have a pastor, Pastor Geuin, who is sensitive to the Spirit of God and teaches and directs us as God directs him.

The church is a crucial part of this Christian life, and you'll hear different doctrines or teachings that say the church is not as important as it is. First off, the church is the body of Christ. Rather than the building, the church is the people, and the people of God are important. When we all get together like this, we can be in fellowship as the Bible commands. We get to edify one another, growing closer to each other and to God.

That's why it is important to pray for our church leaders. We must remember that, just like the rest of us, they're human and need God. They lead us just as God is leading them. They can't do it all; they're called to lead us, but we're called to follow while discerning God's desire for us, too.

So as we wrap up, let's stand.

I already challenged myself and all of you to memorize Psalm 23. I'll do my best to meet with you all again next week and discuss that memorization. But secondly, and more importantly, let's be praying for our leaders, both within this church, the UPCI organization, and the leaders of the world, that God would direct them as they direct those who follow them.

Let us pray...

## Relationship With God
**Published:** 2026-05-14 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2026/05/the-power-of-liberty.html)**

---

Who remembers what we discussed last week?

* ... *

Let us enter this discussion with a time of prayer...

I recently had this thought: Why was Moses chosen by God? We talked about Moses a couple of weeks ago and how God chose him to free the Israelites from slavery in Egypt. We talked about how Moses had his fair share of insecurities, but with God's persistence, Moses became the instrument by which God delivered Israel from an estimated 200+ years of bondage.

Firstly, I think we should understand the relationship between God and Moses. To do that, we will look at an instance between Moses, his siblings, and the LORD.

'Then Miriam and Aaron spoke against Moses because of the Ethiopian woman whom he had married; for he had married an Ethiopian woman. So they said, “Has the Lord indeed spoken only through Moses? Has He not spoken through us also?” And the Lord heard it.' Numbers 12:1-2 NKJV

'Then Miriam and Aaron spoke against Moses because of the Ethiopian woman whom he had married; for he had married an Ethiopian woman. So they said, “Has the Lord indeed spoken only through Moses? Has He not spoken through us also?” And the Lord heard it.' Numbers 12:1-2 NKJV

Moses' two siblings, Miriam and Aaron, are angry because Moses has married a Gentile, someone outside of the Israelite nation. The next verse goes on to say that Moses is a meek man, meaning that he is quiet and submissive. Moses doesn't defend himself; instead, God comes to defend him.

Let's take a quick detour here. Meekness is not a bad thing. Matthew 5:3 says,

'Blessed are the meek, For they shall inherit the earth.' Matthew 5:5

'Blessed are the meek, For they shall inherit the earth.' Matthew 5:5

This is a very different teaching from that of the world. The world says if you want something, then you have to take it. The world says that when someone wrongs you, you should defend your honor, but the Bible says that vengeance is the LORD's (Romans 12:19). In Moses' life, we see this to be true.

God stands up for Moses, reading from the following verses,

'Then He said, “Hear now My words: If there is a prophet among you, I, the Lord, make Myself known to him in a vision; I speak to him in a dream. Not so with My servant Moses; He is faithful in all My house. I speak with him face to face, Even plainly, and not in dark sayings; And he sees the form of the Lord. Why then were you not afraid To speak against My servant Moses?”' Numbers 12:6-8 NKJV

'Then He said, “Hear now My words: If there is a prophet among you, I, the Lord, make Myself known to him in a vision; I speak to him in a dream. Not so with My servant Moses; He is faithful in all My house. I speak with him face to face, Even plainly, and not in dark sayings; And he sees the form of the Lord. Why then were you not afraid To speak against My servant Moses?”' Numbers 12:6-8 NKJV

As I put this discussion together, I won't lie, reading this about Moses made me emotional. Moses had such a close relationship with God. More than seeing God in a vision or a dream- which are wonderful mediums for God to communicate, and one day I pray I can experience those things- Moses instead talked to God face-to-face. We can read in Scripture that " **the Lord spoke to Moses face to face, as a man speaks to his friend** " (Exodus 33:11). The reason this made me so emotional is that I crave a relationship like this with God. I want to be able to talk with him like I would any of my best friends. I want my relationship with God to feel so natural that I don't think twice when it looks like I'm talking to myself out in public. I want that kind of walk with God.

Moses' relationship didn't start like this. We must look at when Moses was first called to better understand the situation. We must look at Exodus 3.

Currently, Moses is on the backside of the desert at Mount Horeb. He has just fled Egypt after killing an Egyptian for harming another Hebrew, for harming one of God's people. What fascinates me is that Mount Horeb is believed to be more than 200 miles from Egypt's border. This means that, while all the oppression Moses' people are facing, Moses is well away from it all.

What a testament to Moses' faith that, after accepting God's call to deliver Israel, Moses returns to Egypt, despite being seemingly free from it all. What this tells me about Moses is that he knew the freedom he had was nothing compared to the freedom God would grant him after he entered into this covenant.

Pastor preached a message some time ago about the freedom that God gives us. Adam and Eve had the freedom to choose whether to eat the forbidden fruit. The choice they made was an act of disobedience; they sinned against God. We have the same choices in our lives. We can do whatever we want and live the lives we want to, but we must understand the consequences of our actions. We cannot live sinful lives while also living righteous lives. We cannot be of the world and of God.

Scripture says,

'“No one can serve two masters; for either he will hate the one and love the other, or else he will be loyal to the one and despise the other. You cannot serve God and mammon.' Matthew 6:24 NKJV

'“No one can serve two masters; for either he will hate the one and love the other, or else he will be loyal to the one and despise the other. You cannot serve God and mammon.' Matthew 6:24 NKJV

So Moses, despite the freedom he carries, given to him by God, ventures back into Egypt with a freedom from God, desiring to give that same freedom to all the Israelites. How did Moses do it? Well, he made himself available. Being as busy as we are, it's easy to just *go go go* ! But Moses said, "Wait a minute... let me take a moment to witness this miracle taking place. This bush- it burns yet it doesn't disintegrate."

What would happen if all of us stopped to witness the miracles inside of us all? We have breath in our lungs. We live on this rock of a planet, which happens to be the only planet that supports life in both our solar system and, to our knowledge, all solar systems. I mean, even if you don't believe in God, you have to admit that life is a miracle.

After Moses made himself available to God, he made the declaration that defines Christian living: "Not my will, but your will, LORD." Now, this isn't the first time Moses has done this. Earlier, in Egypt, he had it all. He was treated as Egyptian royalty. Moses got free schooling, health care, food- I promise you, if any of your parents were given these things on their job offer, they'd take it, no questions asked. But Moses instead decided that what was happening to his people wasn't right. Moses had his mother teach him the way, teaching him about God and what it means to live a righteous life.

I'm not saying Moses always got it right; murder probably shouldn't have been his first inclination, but he still forfeited all he had because he knew deep down that what was happening wasn't right.

So Moses 1. made himself available and 2. put God's will before his own. It's really as simple as that. If we want to be close to God, we have to prioritize him with our time and our sacrifice. We must be on the lookout for what He is speaking into our lives and then be ready to deny our flesh of what it wants to do.

As we close, let us do so in prayer, asking God to help us to grow closer with Him...
