## Send Down the Fire
**Published:** 2025-09-04 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/09/send-down-fire.html)**

---

Today's discussion covers the story of Elijah calling on God to send fire down from Heaven. This story takes place in 1 Kings, chapter 18.

Before I get started, let me set up a couple of pieces of background:

King Ahab of Israel marries a woman named Jezebel; after doing so, he builds up an altar to worship Baal (1 Kings 16:30-33). Ahab does not walk in the ways of his forefather, David; he angers God and causes Israel to stumble in idol worship.

We are told later that Jezebel, Queen of Israel, has been killing the LORD's prophets to further her idolatry of Baal (1 Kings 18:13).

Now here we are as the LORD sends His prophet Elijah to confront King Ahab and prove that He alone is God (1 Kings 18:1). Elijah meets with King Ahab and makes a deal that the king cannot refuse; he has devised a way to prove who is the true God, whether it is the LORD (Yahweh) or Baal. Elijah tells the king to have his men prepare an altar of sacrifice, and both Elijah and the prophets of Baal would receive a bullock to be cut up and laid over the wood. The catch was that neither would be allowed to set a flame beneath the sacrifice, and each would have to call upon their god to create the fire that was needed.

Allow me to pause here to state that Elijah was the only one who not only followed but also believed in God. There were 450 prophets of Baal and 400 more prophets of King Ahab's grove, those who "ate at Jezebel's table" (1 Kings 18:19), doing her dirty work of killing God's prophets, no doubt. So Elijah was 850 to 1; he was the minority, but let it be known that in today's age, those of us living for God are also of the minority. This strait and narrow path we walk is trodden by very few (Matthew 7:13-14).

Continuing on with the story of Elijah... The prophets of Baal get their bullock and dress it, preparing it for the sacrifice. From morning (which was likely at or a little after 6AM; this is when the Jewish morning started) till noon, the false prophets cried out unto Baal, practically begging him to perform a miracle. Elijah watches this and begins to mock them and Baal. Elijah tells the people that Baal must be too busy for them, perhaps in conversation elsewhere, on a journey, or sleeping. It becomes time for the evening sacrifice, and these prophets continue praying and asking Baal to show himself, and still, nothing happens. So Elijah says, "Okay, it's my turn."

Taking 12 stones according to the number of tribes in Israel, Elijah begins making an altar unto the LORD. He cut up the bullock and laid it onto the altar above where the fire would be. He digs out a trench and pours 12 barrels of water into the trench, filling it up. Then, arguably the most crucial part, Elijah begins to pray to God, calling on the God of Abraham, Issac, and Israel at the time of evening sacrifice. Within Elijah's prayer, he tells God, "I am thy servant;" he surrenders to God, giving himself to be used by God. Elijah even makes a prayer of intercession for his nation, for all the people who had stumbled in idolatry and began worshipping Baal, he says, "Hear me, O LORD, hear me, that this people may know that thou art the LORD God." And then, do you want to know what happens next? Fire falls from Heaven, consuming the burnt sacrifice, the wood, the stones, the dust, and the water that was in the trench.

The people of Israel turn their hearts back to God, and the false prophets of Baal, all 850 that once seemed so intimidating, are slain for the evil that they wrought.

The point I want to highlight in this discussion is that Elijah, after being outnumbered by people who were living worldly lives and oppressing him, converted those people, turning them toward a life of holiness. This story teaches us that while we likely won't agree with the lifestyle of people around us, we are still called to love them. These people had forsaken God and chosen Baal instead, but Elijah still loved them because he knew that is what the LORD calls us to do: to love and care for His people.

There are three components to fire: fuel, heat, and oxygen. Let it be said that Elijah provided the fuel, not because God *couldn't* work without fuel, but He simply *wouldn't* . God wants to use us, so when we provide the fuel, or the energy, to do Kingdom work (whether that be evangelizing, disciple-making, edifying of saints, etc), the atmosphere begins to change.

I wonder what would happen if we began to spread the Gospel, not just to those who fit our perspective of how a Christian should look or act, but if we, like the LORD, became no respecter of persons and loved everybody as a child of God. How would that change the way the world looks? We are called to be *in* the world but not *of* it, so what would happen if we found those *of* the world and taught them how to live *in* it, all while following God? I would imagine we would see a decrease in depression among people, or in substance abuse and addiction. We would see an increase in productivity and happy, life-long marriages. What would happen if you finally said, "Okay, God, I am your servant. I want to be used by you. Send down the fire."

___________________________________________________________________________

And he reared up an altar for Baal in the house of Baal, which he had built in Samaria. And Ahab made a grove; and Ahab did more to provoke the LORD God of Israel to anger than all the kings of Israel that were before him. ' 1 Kings 16:30-33 KJV

And he put the wood in order, and cut the bullock in pieces, and laid him on the wood, and said, Fill four barrels with water, and pour it on the burnt sacrifice, and on the wood. Hear me, O LORD hear me, that this people may know that thou art the LORD God, and that thou hast turned their heart back again. Then the fire of the LORD fell, and consumed the burnt sacrifice, and the wood, and the stones, and the dust, and licked up the water that was in the trench. And when all the people saw it, they fell on their faces: and they said, The LORD he is the God; the LORD, he is the God.' 1 Kings 18:20-39 KJV

## The Ship of Theseus Paradox
**Published:** 2025-08-21 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/08/the-ship-of-theseus-paradox.html)**

---

There is a thought experiment in philosophy, a paradox called “The Ship of Theseus.” If you’re not familiar with this topic, fear not, as I will be explaining it through the use of imagery.

Imagine a wooden ship. In my mind, I picture a pirate ship with a white sail and all. Over time, the boat starts to deteriorate. The wooden boards become loose, the sail becomes tattered, and even the steering wheel becomes a cause for concern. Let’s say *you* own this ship; you don’t want to just pitch it, you love this vessel. So what you decide to do is, over time, replace every piece of the boat with brand new components. Those wooden boards are sturdier than ever, that new sail catches the entirety of the wind current, and that steering wheel rotates to the left and right like it’s nobody’s business.

So here’s your new ship, or your old ship, or is it now a new ship entirely? Well, that’s the paradox of it all. Is this the same ship or a new ship entirely if 100% of it was replaced with something else? So, what’s the answer? Well... Because it’s a philosophical paradox, there actually isn’t a correct answer one way or the other. However, it does lead to an even more interesting topic about God wanting to make us new.

Scripture says:

'Therefore if any man be in Christ, he is a new creature: old things are passed away; behold, all things are become new.' 2 Corinthians 5:17 KJV

'Therefore if any man be in Christ, he is a new creature: old things are passed away; behold, all things are become new.' 2 Corinthians 5:17 KJV

The word *creature* used in this verse, according to Strong’s Concordance, comes from the Greek word *ktisis * (ktis'-is), which can also be translated as *creation. * This means that if anyone is in Christ, they are something entirely separate from what they once were.

Just like the vessel we were discussing deteriorates, we too are vessels, but we are the kind of vessels that are made to hold something inside us, in this case, the Holy Spirit. The keyword here is *holy* . The LORD wants to dwell inside us (1 Corinthians 3:16; 2 Corinthians 6:16), but for the Holy Spirit to work in our lives, we have to maintain a life of holiness. This isn’t to say that one cannot be filled with the Holy Spirit, with evidence of speaking in other tongues, if they are not yet living a holy life. I’ll be completely transparent here: I would not have considered myself to be holy when I first received the Holy Ghost; even now, I am striving to live a holy life, but I don’t see how anyone can say they are 100% holy.

We’re using the word holy a lot; let’s first define what it means before going any further.

The following verse in Isaiah calls the LORD holy:

'And one cried unto another, and said, Holy, holy, holy, is the LORD of hosts: the whole earth is full of his glory.' Isaiah 6:3 KJV

'And one cried unto another, and said, Holy, holy, holy, is the LORD of hosts: the whole earth is full of his glory.' Isaiah 6:3 KJV

The Hebrew word for holy used here is qāḏôš (kaw-doshe’).

The following verse in Luke calls Jesus holy:

'And the angel answered and said unto her, The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee, and the power of the Highest shall overshadow thee: therefore also that holy thing which shall be born of thee shall be called the Son of God.' Luke 1:35 KJV

'And the angel answered and said unto her, The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee, and the power of the Highest shall overshadow thee: therefore also that holy thing which shall be born of thee shall be called the Son of God.' Luke 1:35 KJV

The Greek word for holy, used here to describe both the Holy Ghost and Jesus, is hagios (hag'-ee-os).

To be holy is to be separate from the world. When we say God is holy, Jesus is holy, we are saying that He is separate from the world and from the things of the world.

So if the Bible tells us that the LORD is holy — that Jesus is holy — and we know that we were made in the image of God (Genesis 1:27) and meant to live by the example of Jesus, then we, too, should be holy.

Earlier, I mentioned that when I was filled with the gift of the Holy Ghost, I had not yet started the lifelong process of sanctification, of pursuing holiness. God is no respector of persons; He wants to fill everyone with His Spirit, whether they've begun pursuing holiness or not. There is justification, which is repenting of our sins, being baptized by water in Jesus' name, and being filled with the gift of the Holy Spirit. Then, next comes sanctification, which is everything in life after that initial process. Justification is just the beginning of our walk with God; sanctification and striving for holiness are a lifelong endeavor.

Being holy, being separate from the things of this world, isn't always easy. We all deal with this thing called flesh that fights against the Spirit, against holiness, and wants to do its own thing. The Bible frequently talks about this battle (Galatians 5:17; Ephesians 6:12). We are taught about crucifying the flesh, Paul himself says, "I die daily" (1 Corinthians 15:31). There is an ongoing, uncomfortable process of trying to be holy, but I promise that it is worth it nonetheless. The book of John puts it this way:

'I am the true vine, and my Father is the husbandman. Every branch in me that beareth not fruit he taketh away: and every branch that beareth fruit, he purgeth it, that it may bring forth more fruit. ' John 15:1-2 KJV

'I am the true vine, and my Father is the husbandman. Every branch in me that beareth not fruit he taketh away: and every branch that beareth fruit, he purgeth it, that it may bring forth more fruit. ' John 15:1-2 KJV

When something *isn't* producing fruit in our lives, the LORD takes it away entirely. When something *is* producing fruit in our life, God purges it, He prunes it, so that it brings more fruit. Pruning often entails cutting away the dead wood or the excessive growth. One could argue, just like with the Ship of Theseus, if you cut off a branch and it grows back, is it still the same branch? Personally, I'd say it's a new branch entirely, a new creation in Christ.

There are practical ways to live a holy life; it doesn't have to be all at once. What made the Ship of Theseus a new ship was that, over time, it started to look different. First it was the wood, then it was the sail — in our lives, maybe what that looks like is that first we change the way we talk, being careful about the vulgar language we use or how we treat others. Perhaps we don't use explicit language but consume things we know we shouldn't be watching or reading, things that tie us to the world rather than separate us therefrom. By changing the little things first, we can start to notice a big change later on.

I encourage us to pray about what needs pruned in our lives, what it is that we want God to take away so we can be more like Him, living a life that is holy and pleasing to Him (Romans 12:1-2).

___________________________________________________________________________

## King Who?
**Published:** 2025-08-14 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/07/josiah-not-david.html)**

---

Some kings in the Bible are more familiar than others. When I think of the Israelite kings, I think of Saul, Israel's first king after being ruled by judges, who favored sacrifice over obedience to God. I also think of David, the king who took the throne after Saul, who was a man after God's own heart, yet found himself committing adultery and murder, but thankfully, God was merciful to him. I even think of Solomon, the son of David, who was famed for being the wisest man in all of the land (1 Kings 4:30), as well as having a surplus of wives. However, the king I want to talk about today might leave you asking, "King Who?"

In 2 Kings chapter 22, we first read about King Josiah, an 8-year-old boy who has just taken claim to the throne. If this boy were my king, I'd have to be quite honest by saying I wouldn't imagine he'd last long; however, we are told he reigned for 31 years (2 Kings 22:1). Let it be known that these years were not spent on conquering and accumulating wealth, as is often the case with many other kings. King Josiah was different; he walked the strait and narrow, not turning to the left or to the right to the things of the world (2 Kings 22:2).

'Moreover the workers with familiar spirits, and the wizards, and the images, and the idols, and all the abominations that were spied in the land of Judah and in Jerusalem, did Josiah put away, that he might perform the words of the law which were written in the book that Hilkiah the priest found in the house of the LORD. And like unto him was there no king before him, that turned to the LORD with all his heart, and with all his soul, and with all his might, according to all the law of Moses; neither after him arose there any like him. ' 2 Kings 23:24-25 KJV

We are told that there was no king like Josiah, either before or after. This is fascinating, especially given that he isn't as famous as the other kings mentioned previously. The reason for this, I believe, is that King Josiah wasn't worried about fame; **He didn't care if anyone knew his name** . He sought after God and God alone. It is so easy to get caught up in the things of this world—the followers, the likes—but King Josiah had his priorities straight.

Allow me to point towards Josiah's background, towards his family tree. It is easy to argue that a person is only spiritually-minded because of the generations that preceded them. However, I have seen this argument fail in two regards: either someone has left the faith despite having their whole family in church, or someone has grown strong in the faith despite having no family member in Truth; Josiah's case is the latter.

When looking at this next piece of scripture, we read about Amon, Josiah's father, and Manasseh, Josiah's grandfather:

'But he [Amon] did that which was evil in the sight of the LORD, as did Manasseh his father: for Amon sacrificed unto all the carved images which Manasseh his father had made, and served them; ' 2 Chronicles 33:22 KJV

Neither Josiah's father nor even his grandfather served God. There are twelve generations between David and Josiah (Matthew 1:6-11), meaning that Josiah began serving the same God as David a whole 13 generations later. What is even more fascinating is that even today, we serve the same God as our forefathers. The same God that made a covenant with Abraham, the same God who instructed Noah to build the Ark, and the same God that led Moses and the Israelites out of Egypt is the same God we serve today.

Returning to the life of Josiah:

'And it came to pass, when the king had heard the words of the law, that he rent his clothes. And the king commanded Hilkiah, and Ahikam the son of Shaphan, and Abdon the son of Micah, and Shaphan the scribe, and Asaiah a servant of the king's, saying, Go, enquire of the LORD for me, and for them that are left in Israel and in Judah, concerning the words of the book that is found: for great is the wrath of the LORD that is poured out upon us, because our fathers have not kept the word of the LORD, to do after all that is written in this book. ' 2 Chronicles 34:19-21 KJV

This verse says that Josiah *rent his clothes* . Renting one's clothes is a symbolic act that is done to express deep grief, mourning, or repentance. We can see this happen on several other accounts, two of which are when Joseph was sold into slavery by his brothers. Both his oldest brother, Reuben (Genesis 37:29), and his father, Jacob (Genesis 37:34), rent their clothes when they had found he was gone.

I want to have the same sensitivity as Josiah, so that I "rent my clothes" every time I realize I've made a mistake. We should have such a love for God that we care about the things of God. That, like Josiah, we grow saddened when people aren't living out Truth. Inversely, the things that make Heaven rejoice, we should rejoice about too. Luke 15 talks about the rejoicing of angels when a sinner repents. We, too, should be joyous when someone repents, is baptized in the wonderful name of Jesus Christ, or is filled with His Spirit. Too often, we can get carried away with the things of this world, too focused on the things in this life that won't matter in the next. Let me say, souls matter, they aren't just something that exists only in this life. When we evangelize, or spread the Gospel, we bring people from this life into life everlasting.

'And Josiah took away all the abominations out of all the countries that pertained to the children of Israel, and made all that were present in Israel to serve, even to serve the LORD their God. And all his days they departed not from following the LORD, the God of their fathers.' 2 Chronicles 34:33 KJV

One can see that Josiah's repentance was genuine. He not only felt that he should change *his* ways, but he started leading his kingdom in the other direction, back towards God. Josiah led a whole nation back to God; he knew the bigger picture. He may not have had the fame of any of the other kings, but he was a soulwinner. Josiah didn't care about riches; he didn't care about power; he cared about the things of God, and what's more important than God's people?

We cannot be so lustful that we choose to serve ourselves over serving God. Additionally, we cannot become so self-centered that we don't edify others as well. The second greatest commandment is "love your neighbor as yourself" (Matthew 22:39). To love someone is to care for them in such a way that the condition of their soul matters to us. We cannot simply care only for our souls when Jesus Himself tells us to love others as ourselves. The Great Commission, as it is called, is revealed to us in Mark 16:15, where Jesus says, "Go ye into all the world, and preach the gospel to every creature."

Let King Josiah's story speak to you. Even though we may not have a whole nation, there are people God has put in our lives, and it is our responsibility to turn them towards God. If King Josiah, a young boy, a mere lad, can bring a nation to live for God, then we too can partake in the LORD's work right now. Let each of us love others as we love ourself; let us go into the world, all of the world, and spread the Gospel of Jesus Christ.

___________________________________________________________________________

## Being Used by God
**Published:** 2025-08-07 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/06/being-used-by-god.html)**

---

God is omnipotent—all-powerful, infinitely powerful—yet so often we try to reduce His power into something we can grasp. In math, infinity is too vast to write out, so we say something diverges to infinity. If it doesn't, it converges to a specific number. I fear that sometimes God's power feels too hard to comprehend, so we start using the wrong terminology. We say, "God can't use me; I'm too [xyz]." Or maybe, "After everything I've done, there's no redemption for me." But why do we limit God's power? Perhaps you're thinking, "I'm not saying *God* can't; I'm simply saying that *I* can't." Well, news flash, God created you, so no matter what it is that you think is flawed, God wants to use you anyway.

Moses told God that his speech wasn't good enough, and God informed Moses that He had given him the ability to speak (Exodus 4:10-11). Perhaps your insecurities aren't about who you are, but rather about the actions you've taken. Lucky for you, Moses was also haunted by a past, a past of murder. More so than he, Paul, who wrote the majority of the New Testament, killed more Christians than we even know about, and was there for the death of the first martyr, Stephen. And yet, God used both men despite their actions and insecurities.

Someone else used by God was Balaam. He was a prophet through whom God spoke. However, Balaam was not an ordinary prophet; he was a non-Israelite, but God did not discriminate against him. Balaam was called to curse the nation of Israel after their Exodus. From what I understand, Balaam was a diviner of sorts, known to either give curses or blessings, depending on what he was paid to do. Balak, king of the Moabites, paid Balaam to curse the Israelites. Still, after encountering God on his way to meet Balak, Balaam blessed the Israelites thrice (Numbers 24:10). God is not a respecter of persons (Romans 2:11), meaning He loves and wants to use every one of us, regardless of our background.

Peter preached the message of salvation (Acts 2:38), and later he declared it to both Gentiles and Jews (Acts 10:34-43). This is the same Peter who denied knowing Christ three times just after Jesus was taken to be crucified. Jesus warned Peter that he would betray Him, but Peter didn't believe it until after everything had come to pass. When Jesus was in the lowest position He had ever been in, Peter walked away from Him. If Peter can come back from something like that, then we, too, can find ourselves walking with Christ again.

God does not just use people; He used a burning bush to speak to Moses about his plan to save Israel from slavery. He used Balaam's donkey to get Balaam's attention so that he could begin to obey the LORD's instructions. He used a rooster to convict Peter and allow him to come back stronger, refined by the flames of his mistake.

So why is it that we doubt the LORD can use us?

'And when he was come nigh, even now at the descent of the mount of Olives, the whole multitude of the disciples began to rejoice and praise God with a loud voice for all the mighty works that they had seen; saying, Blessed be the King that cometh in the name of the Lord: peace in heaven, and glory in the highest. And some of the Pharisees from among the multitude said unto him, Master, rebuke thy disciples. And he answered and said unto them, I tell you that, if these should hold their peace, the stones would immediately cry out .' Luke 19:37-40 KJV

If we do not give Jesus the glory He is worthy of, He will use the stones to cry out in our place. He is willing to use anyone, not because He can't do it on His own, we've already discussed His unlimited power. The reason God wants to use us is that He loves us. I am well aware that if I don't answer the call, God can find someone else to take my place, but I want to be used by God, I want to spread the Gospel, and see people saved. I want to be used by God because I want to love Him; after all, He first loved me (1 John 4:19).

If you do not believe the LORD can use you, just look at all of those He has already used: someone who had killed a man, someone small in the faith, someone who betrayed Him. Furthermore, He used a bush, a donkey, a rooster, and He will use the rocks from beneath our feet if we do not give Him the glory and the honor that He is worthy of!

___________________________________________________________________________

'And Moses said unto the LORD, O my Lord, I am not eloquent, neither heretofore, nor since thou hast spoken unto thy servant: but I am slow of speech, and of a slow tongue. And the LORD said unto him, Who hath made man's mouth? or who maketh the dumb, or deaf, or the seeing, or the blind? have not I the LORD?' Exodus 4:10-11 KJV

'And Balak's anger was kindled against Balaam, and he smote his hands together: and Balak said unto Balaam, I called thee to curse mine enemies [the Israelites], and, behold, thou hast altogether blessed them these three times.' Numbers 24:10 KJV

## Being Intellectual About the Word
**Published:** 2025-07-19 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/07/being-intellectual-about-word.html)**

---

I recently taught in my church's youth class about the parallels between the sons of Eli, the priest, Hophni and Phinehas, and the sons of Samuel the prophet, Joel and Abiah. I find it so fascinating that in both instances, these individuals were surrounded by the presence of God their whole lives, and yet never made God present in their lives.

I believe a prominent component in the life of a 'successful' Christian is being intellectual about the Word of God. Notice I distinctly used the word 'intellectual' rather than 'knowledgeable.' There is a reason for my madness. Knowledge can be defined as "the collection of skills and information a person has acquired through experience." Intelligence, on the other hand, is "the ability to apply knowledge." Based on these definitions, it is safe to say that intelligence surpasses knowledge because it goes a step further than simply knowing; it involves the act of applying knowledge.

In the case of the very first sin, when Adam and Eve's eyes were opened after eating from the tree. It's the Pandora's Box of Christianity. Knowing this, we must acknowledge that even when we make a mistake and attempt to cover it up with something as temporary as fig leaves (Genesis 3:7), only God can wash away our sins entirely.

I remember when I was in little kids' class at church, we used to sing this song: "Who built the Ark? Noah, Noah." It was great to establish that knowledge at a young age, but there comes a time when we must see that we too are going to be asked to do things by the LORD that seem ridiculous to the modern world; nonetheless, we must be obedient. For instance, to the world, it seems old-fashioned to save ourselves until marriage, but God tells us to, so we should.

In the lives of Joseph (the son of Israel) and Job, both endured great tribulation despite walking in the Spirit. We can read the Bible and know that, but we can also apply it to our lives and recognize that we all must go through storms, and that our God will be by our side, working all things for our good (Romans 8:28).

Knowing that there were three Hebrew boys sent through a fiery furnace because they would not worship the idols of the world is great and all, but what if we took it a step further and applied it to our own lives? If we use our intellect and acknowledge the fact that God stood by their side because they stood by His, and realize He is no respecter of persons (Acts 10:34; Romans 2:11), and wants to be there for us today too, we would have a stronger faith.

The same is true for Daniel in the lion's den; it is one thing to know that Daniel remained steadfast to the spiritual discipline of prayer, but it is another to realize the power that is still found with prayer today.

I want everyone to know that our God came down to earth as a man, that Jesus Christ came and died to pay the price for our sins, and then conquered death so that we could be free in this world and live with Him forever in the next. However, that knowledge won't do us any good unless we apply it to our lives, unless we repent, are baptized in Jesus' name, and are filled with His Spirit, with evidence of speaking in other tongues (Acts 2:38).

Understand that knowledge is important; we need to first read the Word of God to obtain knowledge before we can apply it using our intellect. While Hophni, Phinehas, Joel, and Abiah were likely knowledgeable about the Word of God, given their background, they failed to approach it intellectually, given their life stories. We cannot make that same mistake. Read the Word, know the Word, and be intellectual about the Word of God.

___________________________________________________________________________

'Now the sons of Eli were sons of Belial; they knew not the LORD.' 1 Samuel 2:12 KJV

The term Belial means worthlessness or wickedness.

When "LORD" is used in all uppercase, as in this case, it refers to God. Specifically, it refers to YHWH (or Yahweh), the Hebrew name for God, before the New Testament reveals that the fullness of the Godhead dwells in Jesus Christ (Colossians 2:9).

To know Jesus Christ is to know His Word (John 1:1).

'And it came to pass, when Samuel was old, that he made his sons judges over Israel. Now the name of his firstborn was Joel; and the name of his second, Abiah: they were judges in Beer-sheba. And his sons walked not in his [Samuel’s] ways, but turned aside after lucre [or money], and took bribes, and perverted judgment.' 1 Samuel 8:1-3 KJV

'for there is no respect of persons with God. ' Romans 2:11 KJV

## Patritoism Toward the Place To Come — Independance Day Blog
**Published:** 2025-07-03 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/07/patritoism-toward-place-to-come.html)**

---

As those of us here in America approach and begin celebrating Independence Day this Fourth of July, it would be fitting to create a themed message. I want to start by saying I love America. It is biblical to say that we should love the countries we are from; that is what patriotism is, *loving the country in which one calls their own* .

Several passages of scripture point to the fact that we should love our countries, whether they be our home country (Psalm 122:6) or the country in which we currently reside (Jeremiah 29:7).

'Pray for the peace of Jerusalem: They shall prosper that love thee.' Psalm 122:6 KJV

'And seek the peace of the city whither I have caused you to be carried away captives, and pray unto the LORD for it: for in the peace thereof shall ye have peace.' Jeremiah 29:7 KJV

It's essential to note that one should not bash other countries in the name of one's own, as this is not in alignment with God's Word, nor does it make anyone more patriotic. Jesus taught us to love our neighbors as ourselves:

'Thou shalt not avenge, nor bear any grudge against the children of thy people, but thou shalt love thy neighbour as thyself: I am the LORD.' Leviticus 19:18 KJV

We can view this verse in two ways: to love those in our vicinity, such as our fellow countrymen; but we should also look at it in the sense that we are all God's people, formed from the dust of the earth by His hand, and we should love every human being on earth, no matter where they're from. God is no respecter of persons (Acts 10:34), so I don't think we should be either.

Earlier, I mentioned my love for my country, America; however, what I really want to convey is that, ultimately, this world is not our home. While we should be stewards of what the LORD has given us, I caution each and every person not to become too comfortable, as this world will one day pass away (Matthew 24:35; Revelation 21:1), inviting those who have accepted and obeyed God's Word into Heaven.

'For here have we no continuing city, but we seek one to come. By him therefore let us offer the sacrifice of praise to God continually, that is, the fruit of our lips giving thanks to his name. ' Hebrews 13:14-15 KJV

'For here have we no continuing city, but we seek one to come. By him therefore let us offer the sacrifice of praise to God continually, that is, the fruit of our lips giving thanks to his name. ' Hebrews 13:14-15 KJV

Sometimes we get so caught up trying to make a name for ourselves in this world. What a waste of time, trying to make a name in a place that is soon to pass away. It would be much more beneficial to glorify and spread the name of our LORD, Jesus Christ, to each and every individual we interact with. While this world will come to an end, we get to look forward to a place called Heaven. A place where tears are no longer shed and sadness can no longer rule anyone's day (Revelation 21:4). Where even that of the lowest of lows: the streets, the walls, and the gates, are made of such precious materials, with streets of gold, walls of jasper, and gates of pearl (Revelation 21:18-21).

While Heaven is quite unimaginable, it is so real. During His earthly ministry, the disciples asked Jesus, "How can any man be saved?" To which scripture says,

'But Jesus beheld them, and said unto them, With men this is impossible; but with God all things are possible.' Matthew 19:26 KJV

'But Jesus beheld them, and said unto them, With men this is impossible; but with God all things are possible.' Matthew 19:26 KJV

To be saved, we must inherit the gift of salvation, the gift bought by the price of Jesus Christ when He shed His innocent blood and gave His very life upon a tree, so that you and I could be free.

'Neither is there salvation in any other: for there is none other name under heaven given among men, whereby we must be saved.' Acts 4:12 KJV

'Neither is there salvation in any other: for there is none other name under heaven given among men, whereby we must be saved.' Acts 4:12 KJV

By honoring God's Word in its entirety, including verses such as Acts 2:38, we can be saved and reach the place we have been discussing, called Heaven.

'Then Peter said unto them, Repent, and be baptized every one of you in the name of Jesus Christ for the remission of sins, and ye shall receive the gift of the Holy Ghost.' Acts 2:38 KJV

'Then Peter said unto them, Repent, and be baptized every one of you in the name of Jesus Christ for the remission of sins, and ye shall receive the gift of the Holy Ghost.' Acts 2:38 KJV

To answer the disciples' question, *How can we be saved?* First, we need to repent to God, which means turning 180 degrees from walking the path of destruction to walking the path of life. That means confessing to God our sins, both the ones we are aware of and the unintentional ones we have likely committed throughout our lives. After repentance, one of two things should happen: we should seek to be baptized in the mighty name of Jesus Christ, and seek the LORD in prayer to fill us with His Spirit, by evidence of speaking in other tongues, a language unknown to us. While the order of the latter two varies, it is essential to know that salvation begins with repentance. We also must understand that, after all of this, our walks with God have only just begun. We are called to live like Jesus, spreading the Gospel and sharing this wonderful truth with others.

So, hear me when I say this: we cannot settle for making this world our home when we have a higher calling than this one. While I love America and am proud to live here, my true home is Heaven, and I will strive each and every day to bring others and myself closer to making Heaven our home. I encourage everyone to do the same.

___________________________________________________________________________

## What is Love?
**Published:** 2025-06-19 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/06/friendships.html)**

---

What’s the first thing you think of when you hear the word love? Maybe it’s Valentine’s Day, hearts and chocolates, or Cupid shooting arrows to make people fall in love. Or perhaps you’re still convinced that the opposite gender has cooties—I know I am. But today, we’re not talking about romantic love. We’re going to explore something deeper: the kind of love the Bible calls us to show every day. Allow me to begin with this verse so that we can better understand love:

“So Jonathan made a covenant with the house of David, saying, Let the LORD even require it at the hand of David's enemies. And Jonathan caused David to swear again, because he loved him: for he loved him as he loved his own soul.” 1 Samuel 20:16-17 KJV

These two men, Jonathan and David, had such a strong friendship and such a strong love for each other that Scripture says, “for [Jonathan] loved [David] as he loved his own soul.” How unselfish is this love that Jonathan would care for David more than for himself? That’s the thing about love: it should be unselfish.

I was raised by a mother who believed in the philosophy that love is a choice, and in turn, I have inherited that same belief. She taught me that to love someone means to put them before myself. She also instructed me on the importance of doing this not only in the romantic sense, but in all of my relationships. One thing I find particularly powerful is that God gives us free will. In the Garden of Eden, He allowed Adam and Eve to have options; they could have either put God first, obeying His Word, or themselves by feeding their flesh and eating from the tree. We see this dilemma still today. We can either live for God, walking the path He has designed for us, or we can make our own path, one that is more *self-oriented* .

If you do not yet understand the love of God, how much He loves you, know that He took the Cross to Calvary. He paid the price for sin despite having none Himself. He did not have to, but He did, that is what love is about: going beyond what is expected of us. Jesus helped those who could not help themselves; He helped us. When we were bound by sin, even before we were ever born, due to the flesh and its desire to oppose the Spirit, Jesus made a way out for us.

'Beloved, let us love one another, for love is of God; and everyone who loves is born of God and knows God. He who does not love does not know God, for God is love. In this the love of God was manifested toward us, that God has sent His only begotten Son into the world, that we might live through Him. In this is love, not that we loved God, but that He loved us and sent His Son to be the propitiation for our sins. Beloved, if God so loved us, we also ought to love one another.' I John 4:7-11 NKJV

There is a story I have mentioned before, but I want to repeat it because of how much it speaks about love. There were two boys; the younger had been sick with polio, and the older was carrying him upon his back. An older man came across this scene and asked the older boy, “Is he heavy?” To which that boy replied, “He’s not heavy, he’s my brother.”

Sometimes, we view love as hard work, and admittedly, it is; however, it is so much more than that. Love should not just be considered as labor, something we do just to get it done, but love is unmistakably a gift from God. Love is a fruit of the Spirit, meaning it is a gift from God. We should consider the acts that we can perform to show those around us how much we love them. Even complete strangers should see the love of God radiating through us. They should say, “That person is just a little different,” or “Whatever they have, I need it in my life.” We all need the love of God.

_____________________________________________________________________________________

Jonathan comes from the Hebrew word yᵊhônāṯān (yeh-ho-naw-thawn’), meaning “Jehovah has given.” Friendships are God-given. Notice the friendship that God shared with Adam and Eve and how it still exists today. Despite the mistake that they made, God still loved them. We need the kind of friendships where, when one person stumbles, the other is still willing to help them back up, the kind of love that is not seasonal but consistent in both raging storms and sunny skies. The type of love that we can only get from Jesus Christ.


---

## The Peace of God
**Published:** 2025-06-12 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/06/the-peace-of-god.html)**

---

'Enter ye in at the strait gate: for wide is the gate, and broad is the way, that leadeth to destruction, and many there be which go in thereat: because strait is the gate, and narrow is the way, which leadeth unto life, and few there be that find it.' Matthew 7:13-14 KJV

'Enter ye in at the strait gate: for wide is the gate, and broad is the way, that leadeth to destruction, and many there be which go in thereat: because strait is the gate, and narrow is the way, which leadeth unto life, and few there be that find it.' Matthew 7:13-14 KJV

The strait and narrow is a topic that I love to discuss and have discussed many times before. However, today I want to discuss one topic that I am still becoming more familiar with, a topic that is crucial to finding the LORD's Will for one's life. That is the topic of peace.

I have always reinforced the uncomfortable lifestyle of a believer. Recently, I have heard messages and advice about pursuing a life of peace when determining God’s Will. On all accounts, people have told me that if there is no peace within a decision, then that decision likely is not the Will of God. My pastor told me a story about a time he went to pick out a truck. After test-driving it, despite there being nothing seemingly wrong with it, he just did not feel at peace with that particular vehicle and went on to look at another. I tell that story about my pastor to illustrate that even when we think God doesn't care about some of our decisions, He absolutely does, because He loves and cares about us. I am reminded of the following verse:

“And the peace of God, which passeth all understanding, shall keep your hearts and minds through Christ Jesus.” Philippians 4:7 KJV

“And the peace of God, which passeth all understanding, shall keep your hearts and minds through Christ Jesus.” Philippians 4:7 KJV

The fruits of the Spirit, as described in Galatians 5:22-23, are love, joy, *peace* ... before we even finish going through the whole list, we run into the word peace. Where God's presence is, so are these fruits. Which means if we find ourselves in chaotic situations, we should ask ourselves, *At what point did I forsake the Will of God?*

At first glance, when comparing the uncomfortable and peaceful, I was under the impression they conflict with one another, but I know the Word of God does not contradict itself. I have mentioned the imagery of the strait and narrow path before. It can sometimes be a windy path, and we have to trust God to lead us through it. The Bible tells us in John 1:1 that God is the Word. Psalm 119:105 goes on to say that it is the Word that lightens our path, and in this illustration, lightens the strait and narrow road. So, where does peace come in? Along the sides of the road, imagine there are these spiritual guardrails that ensure you do not stray into the brush. By falling away from the strait and narrow, there is a strong likelihood that one wanders into the broader path, the path that leads to destruction. These guardrails are what I would define as the peace of God. If we are too radical and traversing a life that is described as uncomfortable, we might not be in the Will of God at all. Instead, we might just find ourselves in the brush off the path. However, by staying within the guardrails of peace, we see the perfect combination of an uncomfortable yet peaceful life working for the Kingdom.

I encourage everyone to invite that friend to church. It might be uncomfortable, but the peace that will come with it is undoubtedly of God. Testify to that coworker or family member about the good that God has done in your life, and feel the rushing wave of peace wash over you.

While we are called to live uncomfortable lives as believers—lives that require us to decrease so God can increase (John 3:30), to crucify our flesh (Galatians 5:24), and bear our cross (Matthew 16:24)—we are also blessed with the peace of God.

## Fear the LORD
**Published:** 2025-06-03 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/06/fear-lord.html)**

---

Throughout God's Word, one can find scriptures that talk about fearing the LORD:

'The fear { *yir'â* } of the LORD is the beginning of wisdom: A good understanding have all they that do his commandments: His praise endureth for ever.' Psalm 111:10 KJV

'The fear { *yir'â* } of the LORD is the beginning of wisdom: A good understanding have all they that do his commandments: His praise endureth for ever.' Psalm 111:10 KJV

'Be not wise in thine own eyes: Fear { *yārē'* } the LORD, and depart from evil.' Proverbs 3:7 KJV

'Be not wise in thine own eyes: Fear { *yārē'* } the LORD, and depart from evil.' Proverbs 3:7 KJV

'The fear { *yir'â* } of the LORD is the beginning of wisdom: And the knowledge of the holy is understanding.' Proverbs 9:10 KJV

'The fear { *yir'â* } of the LORD is the beginning of wisdom: And the knowledge of the holy is understanding.' Proverbs 9:10 KJV

*The Hebrew word for fear, as in the noun, is *yir'â* (yir-aw'), while *yārē* (yaw-ray') means to fear, or the verb for fear.

In all these cases, fear does not simply mean "being frightened by," but it means "being in awe of." For example, imagine looking upon a dormant volcano. It would be quite a fearful sight, not because one is scared of the volcano, which is no longer active, but rather, they are in awe of the mighty and magnificent standing before them. While God's power is most definitely active and greater than any volcano, no matter the status, He would never hurt us, His children.

The fear we are supposed to hold regarding God is that of respect, just as we would our parents. I would never say that I fear my mom, and I hope no one would say that. However, I know that there are consequences to undermining her, just as there are for any parent (e.g., a lack of trust). As we grow older, we should still respect our parents' role in our lives, though we will likely gain freedom from them as we age. However, God is the same yesterday, today, and forever (Hebrews 13:8); we should submit to Him no matter how old we get.

Another way to look at fear is being sensitive to the fact that what we have is so precious.

“Wherefore, my beloved, as ye have always obeyed, not as in my presence only, but now much more in my absence, work out your own salvation with fear { *phobos* } and trembling. For it is God which worketh in you both to will and to do of his good pleasure.” Philippians 2:12-13 KJV

“Wherefore, my beloved, as ye have always obeyed, not as in my presence only, but now much more in my absence, work out your own salvation with fear { *phobos* } and trembling. For it is God which worketh in you both to will and to do of his good pleasure.” Philippians 2:12-13 KJV

*The Greek word for fear, as in the noun, is *phobos* .

Here, Paul is writing to the Philippians that they should remain sensitive to the working of God even when he himself is not present. There's a good teaching there that we should be consistent Christians, not only within the walls of the church and around our pastors, but in our homes and at our workplaces too.

A way that helps to understand sensitivity better is when I am holding my niece or nephew; there is something so precious about children. In addition to that preciousness is the fear that I could drop them. The same idea can be made in regards to a vase; it is so fragile and we would not want to let go of it. When we fear God, we want to be sensitive to the work He is doing in our lives to never lose it from our grasp (Nehemiah 6:3). We want to ensure that we are following God's plan and the path He has directed for us (Proverbs 3:6).

At the end of the day, God has respect for us, so we should respect Him as well. The LORD gives us free will; we do not have to follow His Word. God gave Adam and Eve the power of choice. He gave them instructions not to eat from one particular tree in the Garden of Eden, but He left it there to ensure they still could choose for themselves. When we fall short of His glory (Romans 3:23), He still accepts us back into His house as if we never left, ring, robe, and all (Luke 15:21-24).

We can further understand God's respect for us when we read the following verse of scripture:

“I will praise thee; for I am fearfully { *yārē'* } and wonderfully made: Marvellous are thy works; And that my soul knoweth right well.” Psalm 139:14 KJV

“I will praise thee; for I am fearfully { *yārē'* } and wonderfully made: Marvellous are thy works; And that my soul knoweth right well.” Psalm 139:14 KJV

When God created us, He did so with respect and awe-inspiration, so that we would understand how important our lives are, not just as individuals but the lives of our brothers and sisters in Christ as well. We should not only give God the respect He deserves but also do so by showing respect to those around us.

## Crucifying the Flesh
**Published:** 2025-05-26 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/05/crucifying-flesh.html)**

---

I want to talk about a very important act that all Christians must go through. Beyond the acts it takes for salvation: repenting, being baptized in Jesus' name, and filled with His spirit, there is an act that lasts the rest of one's life. That act is crucifying the flesh.

Flesh, as described in its original Greek translation from which the New Testament was written ( *sarx* ), refers to our carnal or worldly desires as human beings, our human nature. It is important to note that our will is not the same as God's Will. In the LORD's prayer, one of the concepts that Jesus teaches His disciples to pray is, "Thy will be done in earth as it is in heaven" (Matthew 6:10-13). Not only should we pray that God's Will be done, but we need to recognize what I have already stated, that His Will is different than our will.

'For my thoughts are not your thoughts, neither are your ways my ways, saith the LORD. For as the heavens are higher than the earth, so are my ways higher than your ways, and my thoughts than your thoughts.' Isaiah 55:8-9 KJV

'For my thoughts are not your thoughts, neither are your ways my ways, saith the LORD. For as the heavens are higher than the earth, so are my ways higher than your ways, and my thoughts than your thoughts.' Isaiah 55:8-9 KJV

Not only is God's Will different from ours, but it is better too. He sees all and knows all, preparing us for every season of life, which is why we need to deny ourselves and follow Him.

The term of crucifying the flesh, in its original Greek meaning ( *stauroō* ), means to destroy the power thereof entirely. Admittedly, this is not a comfortable process by any means, but looking at the teaching of the strait and narrow (Matthew 7:13-14), the life of a Christian is not meant to be comfortable. There is a consistent teaching that I have heard throughout my life, the teaching of God putting us through some storms and refining us in the furnace so that He can teach us and mold us into a new creation (2 Corinthians 5:17). However, no matter how strong the wind and waves become, Christ has power over them (Luke 8:22-24). No matter how hot the flames get, He is our fourth man in the fire (Daniel 3:19-25).

Furthering the matter of being crucified,

'But God forbid that I should glory, save in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, by whom the world is crucified unto me, and I unto the world.' Galatians 6:14 KJV

'But God forbid that I should glory, save in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, by whom the world is crucified unto me, and I unto the world.' Galatians 6:14 KJV

In other translations, glory is replaced with boast. Paul says here that we should not glorify anything unless it points to the Cross. It was on the Cross that Jesus was crucified, a Roman tradition done unto those who have committed wrongdoings. This was done unto Jesus despite Him having never sinned. The part of this verse I want to focus on is the second half, "...by whom the world is crucified unto me, and I unto the world." Through Christ's sacrifice on the Cross, the world was separated from man and man from the world. This is what holiness is: a setting apart from the world. To be holy, we have to be separate from the things of this world because the things of this world are not of God.

'For the flesh lusteth against the Spirit, and the Spirit against the flesh: and these are contrary the one to the other: so that ye cannot do the things that ye would.' Galatians 5:17 KJV

'For the flesh lusteth against the Spirit, and the Spirit against the flesh: and these are contrary the one to the other: so that ye cannot do the things that ye would.' Galatians 5:17 KJV

A constant battle is taking place, the battle between the flesh , human nature, and desires, and the Spirit , the Will, and the nature of God.

'For they that are after the flesh do mind the things of the flesh; but they that are after the Spirit the things of the Spirit.' Romans 8:5 KJV

'For they that are after the flesh do mind the things of the flesh; but they that are after the Spirit the things of the Spirit.' Romans 8:5 KJV

We cannot be both in the will of the flesh and in the Will of God. The Bible says that "No man can serve two masters" (Matthew 6:24). We cannot serve ourselves while also being servants unto God, once again, our wills do not allign.

'And they that are Christ's have crucified the flesh with the affections and lusts.' Galatians 5:24 KJV

'And they that are Christ's have crucified the flesh with the affections and lusts.' Galatians 5:24 KJV

Those who love Christ and are of Christ must be holy, separate from their human nature, by crucifying their flesh.

'I protest by your rejoicing which I have in Christ Jesus our Lord, I die daily.' 1 Corinthians 15:31 KJV

Paul is saying that he dies daily, meaning he goes through the uncomfortable process every day of crucifying his flesh, dying to his flesh so that he can be the new creature that Christ has called him and all of us to be (2 Corinthians 5:17).

'He must increase, but I must decrease.' John 3:30 KJV

'He must increase, but I must decrease.' John 3:30 KJV

Above all, the point I am trying to make is that to serve God, we must decrease so that He can receive the increase. We do not spread the Gospel for our glory, but rather for His glory. If no one ever remembers our name, pray that they remember the message. The life of a Christian is not in alignment with that of the world. The world promises us fame, riches, and glory, but the life of a Christian promises us eternal life if we follow the Will of God, even when it means living humbly and out of the spotlight. Christ is calling us all to follow after Him; all it takes is denying ourselves and carrying our burdens just as He carried the Cross to Calvary. I want to end on this piece of scripture:

'Then said Jesus unto his disciples, If any man will come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me. For whosoever will save his life shall lose it: and whosoever will lose his life for my sake shall find it. For what is a man profited, if he shall gain the whole world, and lose his own soul? or what shall a man give in exchange for his soul?' Matthew 16:24-26 KJV

'Then said Jesus unto his disciples, If any man will come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me. For whosoever will save his life shall lose it: and whosoever will lose his life for my sake shall find it. For what is a man profited, if he shall gain the whole world, and lose his own soul? or what shall a man give in exchange for his soul?' Matthew 16:24-26 KJV

## Obedience > Sacrifice
**Published:** 2025-05-18 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/05/obedience-sacrifice.html)**

---

In the Bible, we can read about the lives of several kings, including David, a man after God's own heart, a man of obedience. We also read about King Saul, David's predecessor, who too followed God, but not as he should have. Saul was a man of action, to the point where he acted before thinking, the opposite of what the Bible instructs us to do (James 1:19-20). In 1 Samuel, chapter 13, we can begin to see the downfall of King Saul. Samuel, God's chosen prophet, instructs Saul to wait for him in a place called Gilgal, where he would receive further instructions from the LORD. Saul waits and waits, feeling like Samuel is taking too long. This is important because, at times, it may seem as though God has forgotten about us or moved on to a different matter; however, the LORD's timing is perfect, and His ways are higher than our ways (Isaiah 55:8-9). So Saul grows impatient and offers a burnt offering to God, disobeying God and prioritizing the concept of sacrifice over obedience. Samuel scolds Saul and tells him that the LORD wants a man after God's own heart to lead Israel, and Saul and his family cannot rule over Israel; a new king will need to rise up and take his place.

Later on in the book of 1 Samuel, in chapter 15, Saul once again follows after his own heart rather than God's. Samuel instructed Saul to attack the city of Amalek, and that he was not to spare any of the people or animals thereof. Saul not only spares sheep, oxen, fatlings, and rams, but he also spares the king of that city, King Agag. When Samuel finds out, he is furious and asks Saul why he disobeyed the voice of the LORD. Saul explains that he wanted to make sacrifices to God and chose to spare these animals to do so. It was Samuel's reply that really opened my eyes and encouraged me to speak on this.

“And Samuel said, Hath the LORD as great delight in burnt offerings and sacrifices, As in obeying the voice of the LORD? Behold, to obey is better than sacrifice, And to hearken than the fat of rams.” 1 Samuel 15:22 KJV

“And Samuel said, Hath the LORD as great delight in burnt offerings and sacrifices, As in obeying the voice of the LORD? Behold, to obey is better than sacrifice, And to hearken than the fat of rams.” 1 Samuel 15:22 KJV

Samuel shatters Saul's entire belief system, which holds that it is better to sacrifice than to obey God. Saul thought he knew better than the LORD, so instead of obeying him, he tried to do things his way. As I have already mentioned, His ways are above our ways and His thoughts above our thoughts (Isaiah 55:8-9). This verse instructs us that it is better to obey God's voice than to try to do things our way, even if we are still giving ourselves to God. If a pastor believed he could preach a sermon to his congregation that differed from the one God had already instructed him to preach, he would be sure to fail because the orchestration of God's divine Will has already been disrupted due to disobedience. I use this example to illustrate that even talented and compassionate people who want to move the Kingdom forward will fail without the help of the Ruler of that Kingdom. The best way to spread the Gospel and love of Jesus Christ is to obey His voice when He speaks into our hearts.

Do not get me wrong, sacrifice is necessary, but I wonder if we could ponder this question: How often are we sacrificing to compensate for our lack of obedience?

It is easy to give to God and set our own standards for what we should do as Christians. With this mindset, it is easy not only to make sacrifices at the wrong time but also to make them for the wrong things. Sometimes messages are not meant to be heard yet, so sacrificing time to share them with people without first seeking God's instruction is dangerous. In other instances, it is quite possible to sacrifice things not meant to be sacrificed, such as energy invested in specific projects and tasks that the LORD never called you to. This energy would be better spent fulfilling His work, but to find what the work is, we need to seek Him in prayer. We cannot know God's Will unless He chooses to share it with us; however, He will not choose to share it if we do not first come to Him and ask. God may not reveal the entirety of His plan to us, but His word is a lamp unto our feet and a light unto our path (Psalm 119:105). We are not always meant to see the whole road because otherwise it would be easy to try and traverse it on our own, going at our own pace rather than God's pace. So let God lead you, ask that He lighten your path so that you can walk with Him, be used by Him, and learn to prioritize obedience to Him over everything else in life.

## Quit Using Your Babylonian Names
**Published:** 2025-05-10 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/05/quit-using-your-babylonian-names.html)**

---

The Book of Daniel talks about King Nebuchadnezzar, the king of Babylon, taking over Jerusalem by force.

"In the third year of the reign of Jehoiakim king of Judah came Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon unto Jerusalem, and besieged it." Daniel 1:1 KJV

"In the third year of the reign of Jehoiakim king of Judah came Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon unto Jerusalem, and besieged it." Daniel 1:1 KJV

Nebuchadnezzar wanted to take the people thereof and teach them his people's beliefs and customs, as a way of indoctrinating them. There were four boys in particular that Nebuchadnezzar chose: Daniel, Hananiah, Mishael, and Azariah. These boys had been given these names by their families. It was customary in Jewish culture for sons to be named after their fathers, which very well could have been the case for any of these boys. However, the king wanted to give them new names, Babylonian names for them to fit into this "new world." So Daniel was called Belteshazzar, and Hananiah, Mishael, and Azariah were called Shadrach, Meshach, and Abed-nego.

"Now among these were of the children of Judah, Daniel, Hananiah, Mishael, and Azariah: unto whom the prince of the eunuchs gave names: for he gave unto Daniel the name of Belteshazzar; and to Hananiah, of Shadrach; and to Mishael, of Meshach; and to Azariah, of Abed-nego." Daniel 1:6-7 KJV

"Now among these were of the children of Judah, Daniel, Hananiah, Mishael, and Azariah: unto whom the prince of the eunuchs gave names: for he gave unto Daniel the name of Belteshazzar; and to Hananiah, of Shadrach; and to Mishael, of Meshach; and to Azariah, of Abed-nego." Daniel 1:6-7 KJV

These children of Israel made it up in their minds that while this world wanted to give them a new name and identity, they would not stray from being the children of God they were. The Bible says they were rewarded for their faithfulness to God, and He stuck close to them, protecting them and setting them apart from those of the world.

"And the king communed with them; and among them all was found none like Daniel, Hananiah, Mishael, and Azariah: therefore stood they before the king. And in all matters of wisdom and understanding, that the king enquired of them, he found them ten times better than all the magicians and astrologers that were in all his realm. " Daniel 1:19-20 KJV

The world would like to call us many things in today's modern era. At the very least, they call us *sinners* . In other instances, they call us by our past mistakes.

You have likely heard them say:

"Do you see that person over there? I heard they are a *sinner* ."

"Oh, her? How could I not have heard? She even looks like a *sinner* ."

"Oh, him over there? Yeah, I heard about him being a *sinner* too last week, but I am not even surprised."

In all these instances, *sinner* can be replaced with any of the names the world tries to give us. However, worse than the world calling us names is when we claim these false names for ourselves.

We say things like:

"I am so worthless, who could ever like me?"

"I look so ugly, right now... I look so ugly all the time."

"I could have gotten a better grade if I were not so stupid."

I have mentioned before, and I will say again, that our image matters. We are made in God's image. When we poorly draw ourselves, whether it be the way we look, our unique personality, or even another person's looks and personality, we are actually drawing the Creator of said image in that same way.

Picture this: you have just created your most magnificent piece of art and are showing it off to anyone willing to listen, when suddenly someone says, "Wow, that's so ugly." It would come as a shock to me if your feelings were not hurt. So, imagine how God must feel when we do the same thing to His artwork.

"So God created man in his own image, in the image of God created he him; male and female created he them." Genesis 1:27 KJV

"So God created man in his own image, in the image of God created he him; male and female created he them." Genesis 1:27 KJV

While the world wants to point out our wrongdoings, God wants to point out the blood that covers said mistakes. I am guilty of using my Babylonian name, the name the world likes to call me, when I think of who I am. However, God will remind all of us who we are. We are children of the King, not Nebuchadnezzar but Jesus Christ. So today I want to say, quit using your Babylonian name. Daniel, the LORD wants to give you a new name. Hananiah, Mishael, and Azariah, you are God's chosen people; do not conform to the names this world wants to give you. You are not worthless, Daniel; you are valuable to the Kingdom of God. Hananiah, you are beautiful today and every day. You are not stupid, Meshael; you have a mind designed by God Himself. Azariah, the world may call you a *sinner,* but God calls you His own. God calls us all to be one of His chosen people, so stop listening to those like Nebuchadnezzar and start listening to Jesus Christ.

## Valleys and Mountains (Praise)
**Published:** 2025-04-26 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/04/valleys-and-mountains-praise.html)**

---

I have to be a little personal as I begin this blog. I am currently in a season of mountains. I just got back from a youth conference last weekend, where I saw God move in the lives of so many people. So many individuals experienced God for the first time in their lives- whether they were invited by their local church or a friend, I am not sure, but I am for certain that it was not a coincidence that they were in that place, but rather a divine work from the LORD. Additionally, after getting back from said conference, we celebrated Resurrection Sunday, reminding us all to remember what Christ did for us, not just for that day, but every day of our lives.

Once we got settled back into our routine schedules, I went back to school, which had me worried after missing a couple of days from the conference and already not having the easiest time in a couple of my classes. However, while I was at the conference, I felt the LORD telling me that He is greater than all things, which is biblical. In this particular case, He was putting it on my heart that He is greater than my GPA. So when I got back to class, the professor of one of the classes I am struggling with told me that he was dropping the quiz I missed from being away at the conference. I knew the lowest quiz grade got dropped according to the syllabus, but he told me this would be dropped in addition to what was already dropped. I will admit this may sound silly to an outsider or someone who is still young in the faith, but God truly blessed me with this because this is a course requirement for my major that I have been stressing about more than I should have been. Let this be a reminder that nothing is too great or too small for the LORD our God, Jesus Christ.

This week, I also had the opportunity to give my testimony at my church. I had been nervous in the month leading up to it, but more so on the day to come. Overall, it went very well. I had friends and strangers alike who were moved by the power of God in my life. It is important to know that the Bible says God is no respecter of persons, which means that what He has done for me and all of those you see around you, He can do for you too. We are all called to give our testimony of God's goodness in our lives, and one thing I struggled with was the voice of the Enemy saying that what I have experienced is not worthy of a testimony. However, in the moments of meditation on Scripture and in prayer, the LORD reminded me that we all have a testimony that signifies His goodness. Once again, nothing is too great or too small for our God to use- His standards far exceed ours- His ways are above our ways- so we cannot measure people or their lives according to what we believe, but instead trust in the LORD.

I have said all this to say, praising God is easy in the mountains- I can easily give God thanks for the goodness I feel because that goodness is so prominent and visible at the very moment. However, I have been asking God to help me keep this mentality in the valley that I will soon find myself in. I have mentioned before that life is not always going to be easy, the strait and narrow is where we are called, and it is not easy. To say it even further, we just celebrated Christ's sacrifice on the cross; His life was not easy, and He lived with the constant notion that He would have to die to set us free. Jesus' life was not easy, so neither should ours be. I heard it said on WBGL recently that in the moments where we find ourselves to be very comfortable and where life is going well, we must question why the enemy has not been firing anything at us. If we are no longer a threat to the enemy, then we must wonder if we are still a prominent piece in moving God's Will forward. I stress so greatly on abandoning comfort because it is in comfort that the work ceases, but Nehemiah 6:3, one of my favorite verses, instructs us to ensure the work never ceases.

"And I sent messengers unto them, saying, I am doing a great work, so that I cannot come down: why should the work cease, whilst I leave it, and come down to you?" Nehemiah 6:3 KJV

Whether we find ourselves in the valley or on the mountain, we need to be sure that God is getting all of the glory, honor, and praise. Praise Him, for He is worthy of praise.

## The Cross (Burdens) — Easter Blog
**Published:** 2025-04-19 | **[Read Online](https://jonathan-the-psalmist.blogspot.com/2025/04/the-cross-burdens.html)**

---

A story that I have come across on multiple occasions is that of two boys, the younger with polio and the older carrying the younger upon his back. A man came across this scene and asked the older boy, “Is he heavy?” To which the boy replied, “He’s not heavy, he’s my brother.”

As we celebrate the Easter season, I want to talk about the burden that Christ carried for us and how we are to carry the burdens of our brothers and sisters in Christ.

Throughout each of the gospels, we can read about Jesus’ life, His burial, and His resurrection. Easter, and arguably Christianity as a whole, is surrounded by the very act of Jesus’ sacrifice on the cross. From the very beginning, at the time of Adam and Eve, after sin had entered into the world, a sacrifice had to take place to atone for said sin and every sin thereafter. Adam and Eve tried to avoid sacrifice by using leaves to cover their newly discovered nakedness, but the LORD corrected this by giving them animal skins to use instead (Genesis 3:21). This is an example of a type or a shadow- something that points forward to Christ. Sacrifice is necessary to atone for sin- “for the price of sin is death" (Romans 6:23). This is why sacrifice was necessary in the Old Testament, the New Testament, and even now in the present day. However, today that price has been paid by the sacrifice of Jesus Christ. It was important in animal sacrifice that there were no blemishes or imperfections- they had to be spotless- and Christ was and is our spotless Lamb, having never sinned despite being tempted just as we are tempted (Hebrews 4:15).

"And whosoever doth not bear his cross, and come after me, cannot be my disciple." Luke 14:27 KJV

The Bible instructs us to be holy, or "separate from" the world. We are called to be disciples of Jesus Christ- that is what a Christian is, a follower of the LORD, Jesus Christ. This passage of Luke goes on to say (and I am summarizing here, but I encourage you to read it), do not start something you cannot finish. When we decide to commit our lives to God, we should be sure to understand the full extent of that commitment. Walking the strait and narrow is a lifelong journey, and holiness is a lifelong process and mentality; this life is not for the faint of heart. However, a life with Jesus, no matter how challenging it can be, is so rewarding, and He is deserving of our love and dedication, no matter what the result is for us.

"Then said Jesus unto his disciples, If any man will come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me. For whosoever will save his life shall lose it: and whosoever will lose his life for my sake shall find it." Matthew 16:24-25 KJV

"Then said Jesus unto his disciples, If any man will come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me. For whosoever will save his life shall lose it: and whosoever will lose his life for my sake shall find it." Matthew 16:24-25 KJV

To be a Christian means denying our flesh by saying, "LORD, not my will, but yours be done" (Matthew 26:39; Mark 14:36; Luke 22:42). We are to crucify our flesh, an act that already sounds painful with no explanation, though it simply means to deny our desires and follow after God's desires, to follow after His own heart. While I use the word *simply* , it is not an easy task and requires a constant prayer life to effectively achieve, which is why 1 Thessalonians 5:17 instructs us to, "Pray without ceasing." Earlier, I mentioned that a sacrifice for sin is no longer needed because of Jesus' sacrifice. I do want to mention that just because we do not have to sacrifice animals to atone for sin, we need to ensure we are still giving something up for God. To be dedicated to Christ means that we are willing to sacrifice time, money, and even said desires.

"And as they led him away, they laid hold upon one Simon, a Cyrenian, coming out of the country, and on him they laid the cross, that he might bear it after Jesus." Luke 23:26 KJV

Jesus required assistance carrying His burden... let that settle in for a moment. We all have passions that allow us to be effective in our ministry- the place that God has called us to, to edify others (even if we have not identified them yet). For example, I got into church, or rather Truth, a little later in my life than some others, at the age of 15 years old. This was the age for youth at said church, and in my youth groups was where I began to grow and hunger for God. It is for that reason that I now volunteer as a youth leader after aging out of the youth group; I have a unique passion derived from the life I have lived. However, that passion, that burden I have for young people, is not my burden to carry alone. Sometimes I get prideful and want to do everything myself, but I have to remember, if Jesus needed someone to help carry His cross, then so do I. This mentality goes further than ministry; it is necessary for one's walk with God in general. We need others to help us tread the strait and narrow- it may be walked by few, but that does not mean we walk it alone.

I encourage you to do two things: firstly, find someone who will help you throughout your walk with God, and make sure it is someone you trust; it matters who our friends are. Secondly, help someone else along their walk with God, whether that be sending them a text and asking how they are doing, making the time to pray for them, or both. Always lend a helping hand, especially in the case of helping those who cannot help themselves, a practice that Jesus enacted all throughout His life. One last thing, be sure to remember the Cross- even if you are not reading this on or anywhere near Easter Sunday, the sacrifice that Jesus made should be reflected on every day of the year. Happy Easter.
